<a id="preface"></a>

# Preface

In the literature of Old Scandinavia we meet with something unique in medieval
Europe: a great and rich literature composed before the thirteenth century; clear incisive
prose, and poetry whose stormy music can deal fittingly with the tales of gods and
heroes; and a remarkable clarity of vision, showing itself in understanding of human
strength and weakness and full awareness of the greatness of the issues involved.
Comparison with the literature of Ancient Greece is not unfitting; the mingling of
humour and tragedy in the Icelandic Sagas recalls the world of Homer while it
foreshadows Shakesperian drama. But the appreciation of mighty issues in the lives of
simple folk which we find in them is something as new as the perfect mastery of a
narrative prose style; neither was to appear again in Europe for centuries.

The religion of a people who could produce such a literature as this must be worthy of
study, especially as we can see the results of northern heathenism developing until the
end of the tenth century without interference either from Christian thought or from the
Latin culture so closely bound up with it. The doors between Scandinavia and the East
were still open when this literature came into being, and memories of a pre-Christian
Celtic culture yet remained alive. It is a setting of another kind from that to which we
have grown accustomed, that of Mediterranean influences impinging early on the
Germanic world; and there are great riches awaiting the explorers of the realm of thought
in that complex and vigorous age about which all too little is known. Of men's attitude to
life the literature tells us much, and we must recognise the strength and sanity of it; how
much can it tell us of their thoughts concerning death and the soul? This work is an
attempt to begin the answer to this question, and if it can reveal something of the variety
and richness of the lost religion of the North it will have served its purpose. Originally
this book formed Part of a thesis accepted in 1940 for the degree of Ph.D. in the
University of Cambridge. It was while holding a research studentship from Newnham
College that I completed the greater part of the work, and my first acknowledgment must
be to the College for the generous help it has provided. To 
Professor and Mrs. Chadwick I owe more than can be easily expressed: the discovery of
both the inspiration and discipline of research, and unfailing help, both with practical
advice and encouragement, the whole of the way. I would like also to thank Mr. and Mrs.
J. M. de Navarro and Mr. G. N. Garmonsway for many suggestions and for their
sympathetic interest; and Miss G. D. Willcock, who read the book in manuscript, and
Miss Helen Brown, who read the proofs and assisted with the index, for their helpful
criticism and support. Finally my thanks are due to the Syndics of the University Press
for undertaking the publication of this book, despite the difficulties of war-time, and to its
Staff for the courtesy and efficiency they have shown throughout.

<div class="text-right mr-[5em] my-[1ch]"><b>H. R. ELLIS</b></div>

*December 1942*
:::
:::translation{lang=zh-CN}
# 序
古斯堪的纳维亚文学的一些特质在中世欧洲是独有的：
早于十三世纪作成的海量作品；清晰犀利的散文、疾风骤雨的诗歌韵律，与神祇和英雄的故事相得益彰；对人性优缺点和事物重要性的、非凡的洞察力。
拿它与古希腊文学对比倒也未尝不可：悲喜交织的冰岛萨迦，既让人联想起荷马的世界，又预示着莎士比亚戏剧的到来。
但其中对普通人人生大事的理解、对叙事性散文技法的完美掌握，在当时也是前无古人、此后欧洲诸世纪也再无来者。

能够创作出这般作品的民族的宗教必然是值得研究的，更何况我们能从中瞥见这种北方异教发展至十世纪末期、不受周边基督教思想与拉丁文化影响而结出的成果。
这些作品问世之时，斯堪的纳维亚通往东方的大门尚未关闭，前基督教时期的凯尔特文化也尚未消弭。
这片新天地不同于我们熟悉的世界⸺那个早早被地中海烙上印记的日耳曼世界。
在那个鲜为人知的风云年代里，庞大的思想财富等待着探索者们发现。
这些文学作品处处体现着前人对待<em>生命</em>的态度，其中的力量与理性、我们必然要认识到；另一方面，他们对<em>死亡和灵魂</em>的思考，在这些作品中又能体现多少呢？
本作乃是对这个问题的解答的初次尝试，倘使本书能够揭开这失传的北方宗教的一鱗半爪，那它便尽到职责了。
本书最初是 1940 年剑桥大学剑桥大学授予博士学位论文的一部分。
我在纽纳姆学院担任研究生期间完成了大部分工作，因此我首先要感谢学院提供的慷慨帮助。
我对查德威克教授和夫人感激不尽：是他们助我发现了科研的灵感和方向，并一路给予我切实可行的建议和鼓励，让我受益匪浅。
我还要感谢J. M. 德纳瓦罗先生和夫人以及G. N. 加蒙斯韦先生提出的诸多建议和他们给予的关心；
感谢G. D. 威洛克小姐审阅了本书手稿，以及海伦·布朗小姐校对并协助编制索引，感谢她们提出的宝贵意见和支持。
最后，我要感谢大学出版社的理事会，感谢他们在战时困难重重的情况下促成本书的出版；还要感谢出版社全体员工，感谢他们自始至终展现出的礼貌和高效。

<div class="mb-[2ch]"/>

<p><b>H. R. 艾利斯</b></p>

一九四二年十二月
:::

<a id="introduction"></a>

# Introduction

<blockquote>
  <p><q>Have you no desire to be like that king who was victorious against all whom he fought,
who was handsome also, and accomplished in all things, so that in all the northern lands
there was never his like? He who could grant victory to others in battle as well as to
himself, and who found poetry came as easily to him as speech to other men?</q> Then the
king sat up and seized a prayer-book on the bed, and made to hurl it at the stranger's
head, exclaiming <q>Least of all would I be like you&mdash;you wicked Othin!</q></p>
<br/>
<p class="text-right">Óláfs Saga Helga: Flateyjarbók, II, 56.</p>
</blockquote>
It is typical of Old Norse literature that King Olaf Tryggvason and his successor Olaf
the Holy, striving hard to establish the Christian faith firmly in Norway, had many such
interviews as this with the old gods recorded of them. Even in the eleventh century the
power of the gods was not destroyed, and both the might and the beauty of the old
religion still remained a potent force in men's minds. This one-eyed stranger, of whom
we are told, won his way to the king's bedside by the wisdom of his speech and by the
wealth of stories which he could tell of the rulers and heroes of old time, and it is the
irresistible fascination which the storied past held for the men of Scandinavia in the
centuries following the Conversion that has resulted in the rich treasury of heathen
traditions which Old Norse literature has preserved for us. Even such an earnest young
fanatic as King Olaf the Holy could not close his ears entirely to the blandishments of
Othin.

Yet for all the rich poetry and humour of the mythology, for all the information
vouchsafed to us about heathen practices and beliefs, the pre-Christian religion of
Scandinavia remains paradoxically a subject about which we know very little, It is true
that Christianity came late into the north and was only accepted by the Icelandic
Assembly in the year A.D. 1000, four centuries after the coining of St Augustine to Kent
and considerably more than four centuries after the time when the first missionaries
reached Scotland and Ireland. It is true also that the devotion to the past which is so
characteristic of Norse literature has left us such a record as is offered by no other early
literature in North-West Europe. But against this must be set the fact that those who
recorded the prose and poetry of Scandinavia were men who lived after the coming of
Christianity,
and that for them it was no longer a living faith which they chronicled but old lore from
an age that had passed. It may happen that they know much concerning the heathen
religion and never guess that their readers will not inevitably share that knowledge, with
the result that they leave gaps which we find it hard to bridge, or hints which in our
ignorance we are unable to follow up. Or frequently their interest lies in a different
direction from ours, and chance allusions are made merely to tantalise the curious reader.
But all too often it means that they themselves know little of what they are recording, so
that they either present us with a confused mass of incomprehensible details to sort out
for ourselves, or, more commonly, try to supply an explanation from the background of
thought of their own time. Or if they are themselves creative writers, as was Snorri
Sturlason to whom we owe the great account of Norse mythology in the <cite>Prose Edda</cite>, it is
likely that they may choose to build up a new structure from old material, as imagination
and the desire for consistency directs them; this has resulted in some of the finest
conceptions in Norse literature, but has proved perhaps the most misleading path of all to
those who attempt to discover the nature of Norse heathen thought.
:::
:::translation{lang=zh-CN}
# 引
<blockquote>
  <p><q>难道你不想如同那位国王一般百战百胜，英俊潇洒才华横溢、北地举目无双？不想带自己和同伴打胜仗，不想作诗如说话般信手拈来？</q> 
  随即国王起身拿来床上的祷书，狠狠掷向那陌生人的脑袋，叹道：<q>我如何也不会像你一样⸺阴险的<u>奥丁</u>！</q></p>
<br/>
<p class="text-right"><cite>圣奥拉夫萨迦: 弗拉泰岛书</cite>, II, 56.</p>
</blockquote>

古诺斯文学中常常见到，<u>奥拉夫·特里格瓦松</u>国王及其继任者<u>圣奥拉夫</u>为了巩固挪威的基督教信仰，便留下与旧神的诸多交锋的、类似上文的记录。
即使在十一世纪，旧神的势力也未消灭、旧教的美与威严依然扎根在人们心中。
上文中，这位独眼陌生人能够得人欢心、坐上国王床头，靠的就是雄辩的口才和丰富的古代君主英雄的故事。
在皈依基督教后的几个世纪里，斯堪的纳维亚人仍然无法抗拒地迷恋着那充满传奇色彩的过去，正是这种迷恋造就了古诺尔斯文学中留存至今的丰富的异教传统。
即使是圣奥拉夫国王这样虔诚的青年狂信徒，也无法完全抵挡奥丁的诱惑。

然而矛盾的是，在这些饱含着诗意幽默、异教习俗信仰的神话背后，我们对前基督教的斯堪的纳维亚的宗教仍然知之甚少。
诚然，基督教确实是较晚传入北欧。冰岛议会直到公元1000年才接纳基督教，比<u>肯特郡</u>的<u>圣奥古斯丁</u>的封圣晚了四个世纪，更晚于第一批传教士抵达苏格兰和爱尔兰的时间；
诺斯文学也确实对其过去进行了虔诚的书写，其独特性是西北欧其他早期文学所无法比拟的。
但必须指出的是，记录斯堪的纳维亚诗歌散文的人，都是生活在基督教传入之后的人。
对他们而言，他们所记录的已不是鲜活的信仰，而是逝去时代的古老传说。
当时的人或许对异教依然有所了解，但没考虑到未来的读者未必了解这些，结果导致他们留下难以弥补的空白、或是因无知而无法理解的暗示。
又或许，跟我们不一样，他们其实对异教并无兴趣，随意提及仅是为了吸引好奇的读者。
但更多时候，这些空白意味着他们自身对所记录的内容也知之甚少，因此他们要么只给出一团乱麻，留给读者自行梳理；
要么，更常见的是，他们从所处时代的思想背景出发，试图提供自己的解释。
有时候，他们本身就是富有创造力的作家，例如<u>斯诺里·斯图拉松</u>，我们今日在《散文埃达》中读到的北欧神话的非凡描绘都出自他手。
那么，为了追求想象力和一致性，这些作家很可能会重新编排旧的素材。
尽管这造就了北欧文学中最优秀的一些思想，但对于那些试图发掘北欧异教思想本质的人来说，这恐怕是最误导人的一条路。
:::
::::

::::translation-pair
:::original{lang=en-GB}
The primary object of such a survey is to take stock of the material which Old
Norse literature offers us on these subjects, and to see if it is possible to discern any
consistent body of pre-Christian ideas about the life after death and the relation between
the dead and the living. Secondly, to discover whether any connection exists between the
practices recorded in the prose literature and the poems in the Edda which deal with
mythological subjects. In spite of a great deal of valuable textual and critical work done
on these poems, they still remain for the most part obscure to us, and it is possible that an
approach by way of the information gained from the prose literature might serve to throw
new light on them. It will at once be realised that the main difficulty is that of
distinguishing between literary traditions and records of actual practice and belief.
Clearly a wide knowledge of the history and evolution of the literary forms as well as of
the historical background of the heathen period in Scandinavia and the neighbouring
lands must be necessary before any final decision is given. This work claims to give no
such decision; it is no more than an introduction, to clear the ground for further investi-
gation, and in it I shall do little more than attempt to sift and arrange the available
evidence and to see whether any main lines of agreement be discerned.

Moreover, even did we possess the means to trace back such poems of the Edda, for
instance, as are likely to have been composed in heathen times to their original form, our
problem would by no means be solved for us. It is certain that we should still be faced
with conflicting and contradictory evidence, with complexities and inconsistencies, for at
no time during the heathen period did the old faith attain to such unity in the North as to
bequeath to us an unshakeable body of truths or a logical whole. There are no Thirty-nine
Articles of heathen belief lurking behind the obscurities of Norse mythology or the welter
of strange practices recorded in the sagas. There are, it is true, certain distinctions to be
made in assessing the 
value of the evidence the literature offers; in particular the skaldic poetry, whose
authorship and date for the most part can be determined with something approaching
accuracy and which in some cases indubitably goes back to the time of the heathen king
Harald the Fairhaired, is an all-important source for our knowledge of heathen thought
towards the close of the Viking Age. But having allowed for this, it is possible to lay too
great emphasis on the fact that most of the sources which we possess have in their present
form only been recorded in Christian times, in the thirteenth century or later. The
Conversion of the North meant that a new and absorbing interest came into literature,
although the power of the new classical learning and philosophy was never as potent and
all-conquering in Scandinavia as in Anglo-Saxon England, and the old lore, besides, had
been granted four precious centuries to develop and establish itself more deeply. It meant
also that fresh complications were now added to the already complicated material, as has
already been emphasised. But two things must be remembered: first, that with the coming
of Christian culture the heathen literature was for the first time recorded in writing, and
so caught and fixed in permanent form; and secondly, that there was never any question
of the Conversion shattering into fragments a structure already in existence as a perfect
whole. All through the heathen period belief and thought was shifting and fluid, varying
according to local cultures, developing in accordance with particular influences in
separate localities; and the oral literature that reflected it was shifting and developing too.
The necessary first step in rediscovering the nature of heathen thought in Scandinavia is
to discover how much has been caught up and preserved in the literature we possess, and
to assess carefully the extent of the wealth at our disposal before we trace out its origins.
After that it should be possible to carry the quest further, and in the course of this book I
hope to indicate the most fruitful lines of later study. For the moment it seems to me that
the first great test to be applied is that of consistency.
:::
:::translation{lang=zh-CN}
本研究的主要目的，就是梳理古诺尔斯语文学中关于这些主题的资料，并从中甄别出有关来世与生死的前基督教的思想;
其次，本书旨在探究诺斯散文文学中记载的实践与神话主题的《埃达》诗歌之间是否存在任何联系。
尽管人们已对这些诗歌进行了大量的文本研究和批评工作，但其中大部仍然晦涩难懂，而散文提供的信息或许能够带来新的启示。
不难发现，研究的主要难点在于区分文学传统与信仰实践的真实记录。
显然，在做出任何最终结论之前，我们必须对文学形式的历史和演变，以及斯堪的纳维亚及其周边地区异教时期的历史背景有广泛的了解。
本书并不试图给出上述问题的结论；这仅仅是一篇引言，旨在为后人开路。
而在本书中，我仅将尝试筛选和整理现有证据，并看看能否达成任何主要共识。

此外，即使我们有能够追溯《埃达》诗篇（例如那些很可能创作于异教时期的诗篇）原始形态的方法，我们的工作也不会到此为止。
可以肯定的是，我们仍然会面对相互矛盾的、复杂且不一致的证据，因为在异教时期，古老的信仰在北方从未统一，也不是一套完美无瑕的真理体系和逻辑。
在北欧神话的晦涩之处，或在萨迦中记载的种种奇异习俗背后，并没有一套<q>[^tr-39-art|三十九条异教信纲]</q>背书。
诚然，在评估文献所提供的证据的价值时，确实需要做出一些区分；
尤其是吟游诗文，作为了解维京时代末期异教思想的重要来源，其作者和创作年代大多可以较为准确地确定，有些甚至可以无可争议地追溯到异教国王<u>金发哈拉尔</u>时期；
然而即便如此，我们也依然要重申：我们手中绝大多数的文献都是在基督教时代、即十三世纪之后才记录下来的。
尽管新的经典和哲学在斯堪的纳维亚半岛的影响力和征服力远不及盎格鲁-撒克逊英格兰，留出了四个世纪的宝贵时间去发展深化古老传统，
但北方皈依基督教，确实给当时的文学世界引入了新的方向，也意味着原本就错综复杂的史料又生出了新的复杂性。
但有两点必须牢记：第一，随着基督教文化的传入，异教文献首次被记录成固定的文字形式，从此得以留存；
第二，皈依基督教从未将原本完整的体系彻底摧毁。
在整个异教时期，信仰和思想都在不断变化和流动，因地域文化而异，并受到不同地区特定影响而发展，反映这些变化的口头文学也同样在不断演变和发展。
重新发现斯堪的纳维亚异教思想本质的必要第一步，是探究我们现有的文献中究竟记录和保存了多少异教思想，并在追溯其起源之前，仔细评估我们所掌握的丰富资源，之后才能进一步探索。
而本书旨在指出未来研究最有成效的方向；就目前而言，我认为首要的检验标准是一致性。

[^tr-39-art]: 译注：指英国国教会的《三十九条信纲》
:::
::::

::::translation-pair
:::original{lang=en-GB}
Consistency between different sources may be established in a number of ways. There
may be close verbal echoes, parallel statements and repetitions of the same motif, such as
are likely to indicate literary borrowings by one source from another, or a deliberate use
of certain set fashions in style. When on the other hand we find a number of sources
differing widely in style, content, detail and treatment, which seem nevertheless to be
drawing on the same body
of traditions, memories and ideas, then we have some right to claim an agreement which
is stronger than mere imitation, and which may be held to reflect in some degree the
thought of an earlier time recorded, perhaps unconsciously, in later literature. In such a
case there will be contradictions, misunderstandings and minor inconsistencies, but it
should be possible to perceive a deeper agreement on general lines which outweighs
these. This is by no means to argue for a subjective approach to early literature; on the
contrary the approach will have to be strictly objective and the evidence stand or fall
strictly on its own merit without forcing or twisting the information gained; otherwise
this test of consistency will be a mockery. It is necessary, too, to examine a large amount
of evidence before coming to any conclusion as to the general direction in which it is
tending; but as far as old Norse literature is concerned there is little reason to complain of
the scantiness of the field, which is bewildering by its richness rather than depressing by
its barrenness.

This approach is particularly important in dealing with incidents from the Icelandic
sagas which have to do with the supernatural. While it is universally recognised that the
majority of these are based on the doings of historic persons and on reliable local
tradition, passages which bring in the supernatural element are apt to be eyed with
suspicion and dismissed as fictitious interpolations to enliven the story. Moreover, the
high literary quality of the sagas as a whole means that a form and unity have been given
to their plots which could never have resulted from the mere slavish recording of accurate
facts; and how much then of the heathen background is to be attributed to the creative
imagination and shaping hand of the storytellers who have worked on the original
traditions about local heroes? It remains to be seen whether the evidence for heathen
practices given in these tales gives us reason to believe that they are based on actual
customs and genuine traditions remembered from the heathen period. The test of a
consistent picture agreeing not in small details and forms of narrative but in the
fundamental outline, which remains recognisable and convincing, seems the most satis-
factory guide to demand here. As regards the Fornaldar Sögur, the <q>sagas of old time</q>,
the date of these in their present form is late, and it is impossible to base any conclusions
on their evidence alone. But they are of great value for purposes of comparison, and it
will be found that passages from them sometimes serve to illumine evidence gained from
more reliable sources, suggesting that a good deal of the
material they contain is composed from genuine traditions, albeit imperfectly
remembered, from heathen times.

There are two additional ways in which a check may be kept on the reliability of
literary records. The first is by comparing them with archaeological evidence, which can
sometimes supply us with information about early history when other sources fail, and
which provides a record of funeral customs in the heathen period particularly significant
for any study connected with beliefs about the dead; of this, as already stated, full use
will be made. The other method, to be used with caution, is that of comparison with
heathen practices outside the region under discussion. Occasionally the records of
modern anthropologists give us information about non-Christian practices in other parts
of the world which afford a striking comparison with those described or alluded to in Old
Norse literature, and thus offer additional justification for regarding certain features in the
literary evidence as valuable. Both the archaeological and anthropological material may
be dangerous if applied too freely and uncritically, but I believe that neither up till now
has been sufficiently taken into consideration in approaching the subject of Norse heathen
thought. Unquestionably however it is literature alone which can reveal to us something
of what passed through the minds of the men of a former faith and of a different age, and
particularly the work of the poets. I would echo with conviction the words with which
Snorri Sturlason, the first to attempt a systematic record of the heathen religion of his
people, closes the preface to his Ynglinga Saga:

> The poems, as it seems to me, can least of all be set aside, provided that they are well and truly rendered and are interpreted with wisdom.
:::
:::translation{lang=zh-CN}

不同来源之间的一致性可以通过多种方式来确立。
例如，相似的语言、平行的表述以及同一主题的重复，可能表明不同来源的文学作品之间存在借鉴，或者有意运用了某些既定的风格；
而当我们发现许多来源在风格、内容、细节和处理方式上差异很大，却都体现了相同的传统、记忆和思想时，我们就有理由认为它们之间有着比单纯模仿更牢固的一致性。
某种程度上，这种一致性可以表明：早期思想可能在无意识中给后来的文学作品留下了印记。
这种情况下，必然存在矛盾、误解和些许不一致，但更深层次的、总体上的一致性总是能感知到的，也足以抵消这些差异。
这绝非提倡对早期文学采取主观的研究方法；恰恰相反，研究方法必须严格客观，证据的成败完全取决于其自身，而不能牵强附会、歪曲事实，否则一致性检验将沦为笑柄。
此外，在得出任何有关总体趋势的结论之前，必须考察大量的证据。
但就古北欧文学而言，并没有多少借口来抱怨这一领域的匮乏，史料丰富多彩，远非贫瘠荒芜。

这种方法在处理冰岛萨迦中与超自然现象相关的事件时尤为重要。
尽管学界普遍认为这些故事大多基于历史人物的生平事迹和可信的当地传说，但其中引入超自然元素的段落总是免不了质疑，被当作为了润色故事而添加的虚构内容。
而萨迦整体极高的文学水准，有着绝非机械地记录事实所能实现的形式和统一性。
那么，其中有多少异教背景的描述，该归咎于那些讲述当地英雄传说的说书人的创造性想象和润色呢？
这些故事中描绘的异教习俗，是否足以证明它们源于异教时期流传下来的真实习俗和传统，这点还有待进一步探究。
此时，检验一致性的最佳标准，似乎并不是细枝末节和叙述形式，而在于其基本轮廓是否清晰可辨和令人信服。
比如<u>[^tr-fornaldar|远古萨迦]</u>，其现存形式的成书年代较晚，仅凭其自身内容无法得出任何结论，但依然有重要的比较研究的价值：
可以发现，其中一些段落能够阐明更可信来源中的记载，这表明它们所包含的大量素材，虽然算不上完整复原，但或许也源自异教时代的真实传说。

此外，还有两种方法可以检验文献记载的可靠性。
第一种方法是与考古证据进行比较。
有时，考古证据能在其他资料缺失的情况下提供早期历史信息，并记录异教时期的丧葬习俗，这对任何与死者信仰相关的研究都尤为重要；
因而，如前所述，本书也会充分利用这一方法。
另一种需谨慎使用的方法，是将其与目标区域以外的异教习俗进行比较。
现代人类学家记录的世界其他地区的非基督教习俗，有时会与古诺斯文献中描述或暗示的习俗有着惊人的相似之处，从而给文献中的某些特征提供了额外的依据。
尽管过于随意和不加批判地使用考古学和人类学资料有风险，但我认为，迄今的北欧异教思想研究，都尚未充分考察这两种资料。
当然，到头来无疑只有文学，尤其是诗作，才能向我们揭示先前信仰和不同时代人们的内心世界。
斯诺里·斯图拉松，也是第一个尝试系统记录其同胞异教信仰的人，在其《英格林萨迦》序言结尾处写下此句，我完全赞同：

> 在我看来，抛弃所有，也不能抛弃真正用心创作、用智慧诠释的诗歌。

[^tr-fornaldar]: 译注：[远古萨迦]^(Fornaldar Sögur)是冰岛萨迦体裁类型，指冰岛定居时代之前的萨迦，而非单指某部萨迦作品

:::
::::

<hr class="border-2"/>


::::translation-pair
:::original{lang=en-GB}

<a id="chapter-1"></a>

# <small>Chapter 1</small> <br/> Funeral Customs: The Evidence of Archaeology
> Time which antiquates antiquity and bath an art to make dust of all things hath yet spared these minor monuments.
> <p class="text-right">SIR THOMAS BROWNE, Urn Burial</p>

:::
:::translation{lang=zh-CN}

# <small>第1章</small> <br/> 丧葬习俗：考古学证据
> 时光能使古老之物变得陈旧，又能将万物化为尘埃，却唯独留下了这些微小的遗迹。
> <p class="text-right">托马斯·布朗爵士，《骨灰瓮葬》</p>

:::
::::

::::translation-pair
:::original{lang=en-GB}

<a id="chapter-1-disposal"></a>

## The Disposal of the Dead in Heathen Time

If we desire to know what ideas men held in heathen times about the life beyond the
grave, it is natural to turn first to the evidence of archaeology. The grave is an
uncontrovertible witness; changes of custom, trivial or sweeping, the importance of
funeral ritual in the disposal of the dead, the choice of goods to lay beside or destroy with
the body&mdash;all these it preserves for us, as definite facts that cannot be questioned. Any
collection of literary evidence about the future life must benefit by a preliminary survey
of these facts, to act as a touchstone by which the vague or contradictory statements of
literary records may be tested.

But archaeology may only be regarded as a science as long as it remains purely
descriptive; once we begin to interpret the sure standards give way. It is not only a
question of uncertainty because all the facts are not yet known; for when we leave the
customs and turn to the beliefs behind them we are dealing with the whims and reactions
of the human mind, capricious from its infancy. Moreover, though the mentality of the
people whose customs are being examined may be at an early stage of development, there
is possible contact with other peoples at different mental stages to be considered, and
outside influences may play strange tricks such as no logical scheme anticipates. We
cannot hope then that from the evidence which archaeology can give us from heathen
Scandinavia we shall have material for a definite answer to the question of what was
believed to happen to the dead. But a summary made as briefly as possible from the rich
and complex evidence at our disposal is likely to prove the most profitable introduction to
an investigation of heathen beliefs about the life beyond the grave.

We may begin as far back as the opening of the Bronze Age, that
is, Period I of Montelius. At this time the practice of burying groups of people together in
one grave was giving place to that of separate burials, with the bodies laid in the earth in
crouched position. In the first phase of the middle Bronze Age (Montelius II) coffins of
stone or oak were much used, but throughout all this period while inhumation was the
prevailing rite, the normal form of grave was a stone cist within a barrow. Then in Period
III came an abrupt change, and in certain districts the dead were burned and not buried.
The new custom was destined to live long in Scandinavia, for not until the conversion of
the North to Christianity, about two thousand years later, did it die out altogether. It
appears to have come northward through Germany, spreading slowly but with such
startling thoroughness that finally the old practice of inhumation which had so long been
the only one was, in Period III b of Montelius, completely replaced by the new.[^8-1|\*] When
cremation first appeared (Period III a) the burnt remains were still placed in a man-length
stone cist; but during the next period (III b), as cremation became the universal rite, the
grave altered accordingly, and the stone chamber which had held the body now shrank to
a smaller, box-like stone cist to contain the urn. Mounds, when erected, were smaller;
sometimes the urns were placed in barrows of an earlier period, and sometimes in the late
Bronze Age (Montelius IV onwards) buried in flat graves.

But although at the end of the Bronze Age the change from burial to burning seemed to
be complete, certain graves which date from the transition period between the Bronze and
Iron Ages prove a puzzling exception to the general rule, and show that in certain parts
burial of the dead had either never been entirely abandoned or that it was now introduced
again. In Gotland certain graves of early Iron Age date have been found to hold unburned
bodies;[^8-2|\*] it is thought that these may be due to Celtic influence, but since they were never
scientifically excavated no definite conclusions can be reached. A few similar graves
from the late Bronze Age have been found on the East Baltic coast in Estonia;[^8-3|\*] and there
is a probable one from Billerbeck in Pomerania.[^8-4|\*] Most puzzling of all are the ship-form
graves found in Gotland.[^8-5|\*] Some of these are cremation and some inhumation graves, but
in either case the remains, in urn, stone cist or coffin, are laid in a grave enclosed by
upright stones in the
form of a ship. The date of these graves seems to vary from the late Bronze Age to the
early Iron Age, the ones containing skeletons being on the whole later than the cremation
graves. For other ship-form graves in Bornholm[^9-1|\*] and Latvia[^9-2|\*] a late Bronze Age date has
been suggested, though the evidence is not conclusive.

[^8-1]: M. Ebert, *Praehistorische Zeitschrift*, 1922, XIII-XIV, p. 15.
[^8-2]: O. Almgrcn, *Die ältere Eisenzeit Gotlands* (Stockholm, 1923), p. 3.
[^8-3]: *Ibid.* XIII, p. 5.
[^8-4]: *Ibid.* II, p. 23 f.
[^8-5]: *Ibid.* IV, p. 408 f.
[^9-1]: *Ibid.* II, p. 117.
[^9-2]: *Ibid.* XIII, p. 5.
:::
:::translation{lang=zh-CN}

## 异教时期死者的处理

要了解异教时期人们对来世的观念，首要的方向自然是考古证据。
而墓葬是不容置疑的见证者：习俗的大小变迁、葬礼在遗体处理中的重要性、完好或刻意毁坏的陪葬品的选择⸺墓葬将这些一一保存，成为无可辩驳的既定事实。
要搜集任何有关来世的文字证据，都最好先初步梳理一遍这些事实，以作为模糊与矛盾的描述之试金石。

但是，只有保持纯粹的描述性时，考古学才足以作为一门科学；一旦进行诠释，那些确定的标准就会失效。
这不仅仅是由于部分未知而产生的不确定性，还因为当我们抛开表面的习俗、转向其背后的信仰时，我们面对的便是天生反复无常的人类思维。
此外，尽管被研究人群的思维方式可能尚处于[^tr-whig|发展初期]，但仍需考虑他们与其他处于不同思维阶段的人群接触的可能，因为外部影响可能产生一些逻辑无法预料的现象。
因此不能指望，仅凭异教时期斯堪的纳维亚半岛的考古学证据，就足以对前人眼中<q>死后会发生什么</q>的答案给出明确结论。
然而，尽可能简明地概述我们所掌握的丰富而复杂的证据，或能为研究异教徒来生信仰抛砖引玉。

我们可以追溯到青铜时期伊始，也就是<u>[^tr-montelius|蒙特留斯]</u>第一期。这一时期开始，集体墓葬渐渐转向个人墓葬，遗体呈蜷缩状埋在土中。
在青铜时代中期的第一阶段（蒙特留斯第二期），石棺或橡木棺曾被广泛使用；
但在整个这一时期，尽管土葬始终是占主导地位的葬仪，通常的墓葬形式依然是搭建在墓冢中的[^tr-cist|石室]。
紧接着，蒙特留斯第三期的习俗有了急剧变化，部分地区开始火化而非埋葬遗体。
这一新生习俗在斯堪的纳维亚经久不衰，直至两千年后北欧改宗基督教才完全消亡。
这一习俗大致从日耳曼北上传播而来，缓慢但尤为彻底，以至于到了蒙特留斯第三b期，长期以来唯一的旧土葬习俗最终被完全取代[^8-1|\*]。
火葬最初兴起时（蒙特留斯第三a期），火化后的骨灰依然被放在等身长的石椁里。
但在接下来的第三b期间，火葬成为了普遍的葬仪，坟墓形制也随之改变，过去容纳遗体的石室缩小成了更小的箱状石室，用来盛放骨灰瓮。
墓冢也变小了，有时甚至没有。骨灰瓮有的埋在早期的墓冢里，到青铜时代晚期（蒙特留斯第四期以降），也开始埋在平地墓中。

尽管到了青铜时代末，由土葬转向火葬的进程似乎已经完成，青铜铁器时代过渡期间的部分墓葬却不符合这条普遍规律，令人费解。
这表明在部分地区，土葬要么并未完全消失，要么再次兴起了。
<u>哥特兰</u>的部分铁器时代早期的墓葬被发现存有未火化的遗体[^8-2|\*]；
一说这是受到了凯尔特人的影响，但由于这些墓葬从未被科学地发掘过，学界无法得出定论。
类似的一些青铜时代晚期墓葬还可见于爱沙尼亚的波罗的海东岸[^8-3|\*] ；
<u>波美拉尼亚</u>的<u>比勒贝克</u>亦或有一例[^8-4|\*] 。
其中最令人费解的是在哥特兰发现的船形墓葬[^8-5|\*]；
其类型既有火葬、也有土葬，但无论火葬还是土葬，无论遗骸放在瓮、石室还是石棺中，墓穴周围都会用竖立的石头围成船形。
这些墓葬的年代大约从青铜时代晚期到铁器时代早期不等，其中包含骨架的墓葬总体上晚于火葬墓。
在<u>博恩荷姆</u>[^9-1|\*]和拉脱维亚[^9-2|\*]也发现了其他船形墓，一说其年代为青铜时代晚期，但证据尚不充分。

[^tr-whig]: 译注：请读者自行甄别上世纪辉格史观的残留
[^tr-montelius]: 译注：蒙特留斯各时期对应年份如下
    | 时期 | 年份 (公元前) |
    | --- | --- |
    | &#8544;  | c. 1700-1500 |
    | &#8545;a | c. 1500-1400 |
    | &#8545;b | c. 1400-1300 |
    | &#8546;a | c. 1300-1200 |
    | &#8546;b | c. 1200-1100 |
    | &#8547;a | c. 1100-1000 |
    | &#8547;b | c. 1000-900 |
    | &#8548;a | c. 900-800 |
    | &#8548;b | c. 800-700 |
    | &#8549;a | c. 700-600 |
    | &#8549;b | c. 600-500 |
[^tr-cist]: 译注：特指石板就地围成的小型[墓室]^(cist)，不同于可搬动的棺材
:::
::::

:::::translation-pair
::::original{lang=en-GB}

Apart from such local exceptions as these, cremation remains the general rule
throughout the early Iron Age. Some changes in the treatment of the remains, however,
must be noticed. Formerly the bones had been removed from the remains of the funeral
pyre after burning and washed before they were placed in the urn, but now the grave
goods were burned together with the body on the pyre, and the confused mass of the
remains put into the urn and heaped round it without attempt at separation.[^9-3|\*]
This practice
too seems to have reached Scandinavia from Germany by way of Bornholm. As might be
expected, the next step was to dispense with the urn.[^9-4|\*] In certain Pomeranian graves the
remains were left in a little heap without an urn to hold them, and a still further
development was to scatter the residue of the pyre throughout the grave. The new custom
travelled by the usual road, and was fully established in Bornholm by the second period
of La Tène culture. It also became usual to bend or roll up weapons burned on the pyre
with the dead before placing them in the grave.

In the Roman period, beginning in the first century A.D., Roman culture and Roman
fashions touched even far-off Scandinavia, and under the new influences burial of the
dead came back once more into the North. The Roman idea of an elaborate burial was to
leave the dead man lying in the earth surrounded by everything necessary for a
magnificent banquet, so that the graves of this period are supplied with cups, vessels and
stores of food and drink rather than swords and shields and the panoply of the warrior.
This was particularly true of Denmark, where the richer districts were more open to the
influences of Southern culture than Norway and Sweden, and from this time Danish
funeral customs tended to develop on a line of their own apart from the rest of
Scandinavia; an instance of this is the fact that grave-mounds and memorial stones now
came into use again in Norway and Sweden, while graves in Denmark remained for the
most part flat. Cremation meanwhile continued throughout the period side by side with
burial.

As the Migration period succeeded the Roman one the cleavage between Denmark and
the rest of Scandinavia became more marked, and in Norway and parts of Sweden the
mounds became larger and the dead were laid within them fully clothed, with the
weapons of the men and the full household equipment of the women beside them.
Perhaps the most impressive graves found are those of Vendel in Sweden, where a line of
chiefs has been buried, for the most part in their ships, in a series of graves which seem to
date in unbroken succession from the sixth century to the tenth. Nor were the cremation
graves neglected, for the splendid tombs of the kings of Old Uppsala belong to this
period, and again seem to include ships among the possessions burned with the dead.
Ship-funeral is found in both types of graves from about A.D. 500. During the seventh
century there was a tendency towards simpler funeral customs, possibly due to
Merovingian influences;[^10-1|\*] and in Denmark there was no reversal from this. In Norway and
Sweden, however, the Viking Age reveals a movement in the opposite direction. Not only
the princely dead but men and women throughout the country were provided with both
personal possessions and all the familiar objects of daily life, so that farming implements,
smiths' tools, kitchen objects and all necessary for spinning and weaving and the work of
the house were placed in the grave, and horses and dogs were sacrificed to bear their
owners company. It is to this period that the elaborate ship-burials of Gokstad and
Oseberg, which will be described later, belong.

[^9-3]: <q>Brandschüttungsgräber</q>, Ebert, II, p. 122 f.
[^9-4]: <q>Brandgrubengräber</q>, *op. cit.*
[^10-1]: Shetelig and Falk, *Scandinavian Archaeology* (trans. Gordon, Oxford, 1937), p. 261 1.

::::
::::translation{lang=zh-CN}

在上述地方个例之外，火葬在铁器时代早期依然是主流。不过值得留意的是，处理遗骸的方式有了些许变化。
此前，骸骨会从火葬堆的余烬中取出，洗净后再放入瓮中。
到这一时期，随葬品与遗体一同在火葬堆上焚烧，混杂在一起的残余物被装入骨灰瓮中，或堆聚在瓮的周围，未作任何分拣[^9-3|\*]。
这一习俗似乎是从日耳曼传来，经由博恩荷姆进入斯堪的纳维亚。
不出所料，下一步就是弃用骨灰瓮[^9-4|\*]。
部分波美拉尼亚的墓葬中，遗骸不放进瓮中，而是堆成小堆；再之后就是直接把火葬堆的余烬散布在墓地各处。
新习俗依然走老路传播，并在<u>拉坦诺</u>文化二期的博恩荷姆完全确立。
同时形成的还有将随葬焚烧过的武器弯曲卷起埋入墓葬的传统。

到了公元一世纪罗马时期，罗马文化与时尚的影响远及斯堪的纳维亚，在其影响下，土葬又卷土重来。
罗马式的隆重葬礼是土葬，死者四周围绕着盛大筵席所需之物，因而这一时期的墓葬往往伴有杯盏、器皿以及各类饮食，而非刀剑、盾牌等武士的装备。
该习俗在丹麦尤甚：与挪威和瑞典相比，丹麦较富裕的地区更易受南方文化影响；
自此时起，丹麦的丧葬习俗便开始沿着有别于斯堪的纳维亚其他地区的独特路径发展。
一个典型的例子是，尽管当时挪威和瑞典重新开始使用墓冢与纪念石，丹麦的墓葬大多却仍是平地墓。
与此同时，在这一时期，火葬与土葬一直并行存在。

在罗马之后的民族大迁徙时代，丹麦与斯堪的纳维亚其他地区之间的割裂越发明显。
挪威与瑞典的部分地区的墓冢越来越大，其中死者都是穿戴整齐，男性的随葬品是武器，女性的则是整套居家用品。
出土的墓葬中最给人深刻印象的大概是瑞典的<u>文德尔</u>墓葬群，其中部族首领们的船形墓依次排成一列，从六世纪到十世纪似乎都未有断绝。
火葬墓也未被忽视，<u>老乌普萨拉</u>诸王的宏伟陵墓便属于这一时期，其中似乎同样存有随死者一同焚烧的船只。
自公元 500 年起，船葬就都出现在以上两类墓中。
七世纪期间，葬礼有精简的趋势，或受<u>墨洛温王朝</u>的影响[^10-1|\*]。
丹麦自此一直从简发展，但维京时代的挪威与瑞典却反其道而行之。
不仅王公贵族，各地平民百姓男女的随葬品都包括私人财产和日用品，如农具、锻具、厨具和各种纺织家务用具，以及马匹犬只等牺牲。
下文将提及的<u>戈克斯塔德</u>与<u>奥塞伯格</u>的隆重船葬正是在这一时期。
::::
:::::

:::::translation-pair
::::original{lang=en-GB}
The favourite method of interment of either cremation or inhumation burials
throughout the Viking Age was the howe, but it was by no means the invariable form of
grave; there are a good many examples of stone-formed graves too, and there are also
cases of flat graves dug down into the earth with no howe above. In certain localities the
timbered chambers, found in East Norway in the Migration period, continued into the
Viking Age, with or without a howe; in these the dead seem to have been sometimes
placed sitting upright.[^10-2|\*]

Meanwhile cremation continued in Scandinavia until late in the Viking Age. When
Iceland was colonised at the end of the ninth century we find no evidence for cremation
graves among the early
settlers, and we might therefore be tempted to assume that the custom was no longer
widespread at the time. But the position varied greatly in different parts of Scandinavia.[^11-1|\*]
In Denmark inhumation was practically universal throughout the Viking Age, with the
exception of Jylland, where a few cremation graves may be due to settlers from Norway
or Sweden. In Gotland inhumation is predominant; in Öland the numbers of cremation
and inhumation graves are perhaps equal; in Sweden, however, cremation is in the
ascendancy, except for Skåne, and the same may be said for Norway as a whole, although
here there are wide local differences. A study of archaeological records from the inland
regions there, made by E. S. Engelstad in 1926,[^11-2|\*] shows that in these parts cremation
graves were still in the majority in the tenth century, although by this time the number of
inhumation graves had increased considerably. In the eleventh century the numbers were
nearly equal, with the cremation graves still slightly in the majority. Taking the Viking
Age as a whole we find that the number of cremation graves exceeds that of inhumation
ones, but the difference is much more marked in the inland districts than in the west
coastal region,[^11-3|\*] where inhumation rapidly became popular. There are also differences in
orientation; Shetelig, in his survey of the western region, found that most of the skeletons
lay with their heads to the north, but in the inland region the usual procedure was to place
the bodies facing westward until Christian orientation was adopted in the tenth and
eleventh centuries, and there must, as Engelstad points out, be two different cultures
involved. Although the western region was the first to be converted to Christianity, many
of its inhumation graves are undoubtedly heathen ones, as may be seen from the large
numbers facing north, and since it was from this region where cremation was dying out
more rapidly that Iceland was mainly colonised, we have a reasonable explanation for the
lack of cremation graves there.

Cremation in Norway, then, continued as late as the eleventh century. There is also
evidence for it up to the tenth century in Swedish settlements in North Russia,[^11-4|\*] where in
certain cemeteries
the transition from cremation to inhumation can be clearly seen. A record of an elaborate
cremation ceremony comes from an Arab traveller who claims to have witnessed it on the
Volga in the year 921, in what presumably was a Scandinavian settlement.[^12-1|\*] Evidently the
practice of burning the dead went on in the North until Christianity was so firmly
established that inhumation once more became the universal custom. The evidence of
Norway and Iceland shows that by the ninth century it no longer formed an essential part
of the heathen religion, but the position evidently varied in different parts of
Scandinavia. Almgren[^12-2|\*] comments on the fact that it is in the more progressive and richer
regions of Scandinavia on the whole that inhumation was adopted on the largest scale,
and he suggests that this may be due to the influence of Christian countries farther south,
felt in Scandinavia in burial customs long before Christianity itself made its way there.
The question of identifying Christian graves at the end of the Viking Age is in any case
an extremely difficult one, as there seems no doubt that grave-goods continued to be
buried with the dead after their conversion to Christianity, as has also been found to be
the case in Anglo-Saxon England.[^12-3|\*] Almgren suggests that the flat, stone-lined graves in
which the dead were laid in coffins, found at Björkö and elsewhere, may be the first
distinctively Christian interments.

[^10-2]: Almgren, <q>Vikingatidcns grafskick i vcrkligheten och i den fornordiska litteraturen</q> (*Nordiska Studier til.
A. Noreen*, Uppsala, 1904), p. 310 f.
[^11-1]: Almgren, *op. cit.* p. 318 f.
[^11-2]: Engelstad, *Bergens Museums Aarbok*, 1926, p. 40 f.
[^11-3]: Statistics as follows: in Western Norway, 107 cremation graves to 73 inhumation; in Central Norway 110
cremation graves to 20 inhumation (Engelstad, p. 62).
[^11-4]: Arne, *La Suède et l'Orient* (Uppsala, 1914), p. 23 f.
[^12-1]: Sec p. 45 below.
[^12-2]: Almgren, *op. cit.* p. 319.
[^12-3]: Lethbridge, *Camb. Antiq. Soc.* (N.S.), III, p. 82.

::::
::::translation{lang=zh-CN}
在整个维京时代，无论是火葬还是土葬，最普遍的安葬方式是建造[^tr-howe|土丘]，但这绝非唯一的墓葬形式；
也有许多石构墓葬的实例，以及直接在地下挖掘、上方不设坟丘的平地墓。
在某些地区，民族大迁徙时代盛行于挪威东部的木构墓室习俗一直延续到了维京时代。
这类墓葬既有带坟丘的，也有不带坟丘的，其墓室中死者有时似乎是以端坐的姿势安放的[^10-2|\*]。

与此同时，在斯堪的纳维亚，土葬习俗一直延续至维京时代晚期。
九世纪末，殖民冰岛的早期定居者并未有进行火葬的迹象，不免让人推测这一习俗在当时似乎已不再普遍。
但斯堪的纳维亚各地的情况不尽相同[^11-1|\*]。
维京时期，丹麦几乎只有土葬，除了<u>日德兰</u>地区的部分火葬墓、可能出自挪威和瑞典的定居者之手。
哥特兰以土葬为主；<u>厄兰</u>的火葬墓与土葬墓数量基本持平；
而在瑞典，除<u>斯堪尼</u>以外，火葬正日益盛行；挪威的整体情况也大致相同，尽管有地区差异。
<u>E. S. 恩格尔斯塔德</u>在 1926 年对挪威内陆考古学记录的调查[^11-2|\*]显示，十世纪时火葬仍是当地主流，尽管土葬墓的数量已大幅增加。
到了十一世纪，两方数量几乎相等，火葬墓稍占上风。
纵观整个维京时代，火葬墓的数量是大于土葬墓的，但差异主要体现在内陆地区，而在西部沿海地带[^11-3cn|\*]则是土葬迅速流行起来。
地区之间还有骸骨朝向的区别：<n>谢特利格</n>的调查发现，西部地区的骸骨头部多置于北方，而内陆地区的习俗是让死者面朝西方，直到十、十一世纪改为[^tr-christ-orient|基督教的朝向]。
恩格尔斯塔德指出，这必然涉及到两种不同的文化。
尽管西部地区是最早皈依基督教的，但从大量头朝北的墓葬可以看出，其土葬墓无疑仍遵从异教之法。
并且，火葬式微的西部地区也是冰岛殖民者的主要来源地，这也可以解释为何冰岛缺少火葬墓了。

此后，挪威的火葬习俗一直延续至十一世纪。
在俄罗斯北部的瑞典人聚落，有证据表明火葬习俗至少延续到了十世纪[^11-4|\*]，并可以看到火葬过渡到土葬的痕迹；
一份出自阿拉伯旅人的记录声称，其亲眼目睹了 921 年伏尔加河畔的一场隆重的火葬仪式，所在地或为斯堪的纳维亚人的聚落[^12-1|\*]。
显然，在北方，火葬习俗一直延续着；直到基督教确立了稳固的地位，土葬才重新成为普遍的习俗。
挪威和冰岛的证据表明，到了九世纪，火葬不再是异教的核心习俗，但斯堪的纳维亚各地仍有差异。
<u>阿尔姆格伦</u>[^12-2|\*]指出，斯堪的纳维亚内部比较进步和富裕的地区最先大范围采用土葬。
他认为这或许是受到了南边基督教国家的影响，在基督教到来之前，其影响就已经波及了斯堪的纳维亚的丧葬习俗。
到了维京时代晚期，辨别基督徒墓葬就成了一件难事，因为即使在皈依基督教之后，随葬品也依旧同死者一起埋下，与盎格鲁-撒克逊英格兰相照应[^12-3|\*]。
阿尔姆格伦提出，在<u>比约克岛</u>及其他地点发现的那些内设棺椁、以石块铺砌的平坦墓葬，可能是最早具有鲜明基督教特征的安葬形式。

[^tr-howe]: 译注：原文howe，词源古诺斯语，多用于苏格兰和英格兰北部，特指土堆筑成的碗状坟丘。
[^11-3cn]: 数据如下：挪威西部，107 处火葬对比 73 处土葬；挪威中部，110 处火葬对比 20 处土葬 (Engelstad, p. 62)。
[^tr-christ-orient]: 译注：基督教埋葬死者通常头在西而脚在东，与之前正好相反。
::::
:::::


:::::translation-pair
::::original{lang=en-GB}
From this rapid survey of funeral customs, certain changes in practice stand out
clearly, suggesting that new ideas about the afterlife and the grave must have reached
those who instituted them. The first and the most striking is that from inhumation to
burning in the Bronze Age. Was this inspired by the wish to free the spirit of the dead
from the clogging prison of the body, or by anxiety to protect the living from the harmful
influence of the corpse while it still remained undecayed? Was it, in short, born of love or
fear of the dead or of a combination of the two? And did the new practice reflect a new
conception, that of another world to which the untrammelled spirit might journey? These
are questions which have been long and widely discussed, and practices in many parts of
the world have been quoted in support of the various theories; but nothing conclusive has
yet been said.[^12-4|\*] The evidence for linking cremation with the burnt
offering on the one hand, and with the cult of the sun god on the other, is perhaps the
most suggestive. It is very likely that no one answer alone is sufficient, and that many
different influences are involved; we can only be sure that so radical a change adopted
with such unanimity throughout Europe must have satisfied the deepest desires and
beliefs about the dead held by widely scattered peoples, while at the same time it
presupposes close bonds between practice and belief in far separated regions.
Archaeology alone can never tell us the meaning of cremation to those who practised it;
we shall see later on whether literary evidence can go any further.

At the beginning of the Iron Age there is a further change in funeral ritual to be
considered, when the custom came in of preserving the whole residue of the pyre and
burying it in the grave without attempting to separate bones from grave-goods.[^13-1|\*] Such a
step might imply mere carelessness, but on the other hand it might also mean the very
opposite, a more scrupulous care to prevent anything from being overlooked and thrown
away, either lest the dead should suffer or lest a slight to them should bring harm to the
living. Such meticulous care over the burning of the body is attributed to the Estonians at
a much later date by a traveller who visited King Alfred:[^13-2|\*] <q>There is a custom among the
Estonians,</q> he says, <q>that in every tribe dead men shall be burned; and if a single bone be
found unburned there, heavy atonement shall be made for it.</q>

Whether the reintroduction of inhumation in the Roman period came about through a
change of belief seems more doubtful; it may have been part of the wave of Southern
fashions due to contact with the ancient world, entering the more easily because cremation
no longer represented definite ideas about the after-life. In any case the two practices
continued side by side, with wide local differences, as in Norway later on.

Time last change to be noticed is one which took place late in the heathen period,
during the Viking Age, when the simplicity which had marked funeral rites in the seventh
and eighth centuries was suddenly replaced in Norway and Sweden by a new elaboration
in the choice of grave-goods for all classes of people. The dead were given as complete
an equipment as possible of personal possessions, weapons, tools and household
equipment, while animals were sacrificed to accompany them; and in the funerals of the greatest folk, whether men or
women, the array of treasures and the sacrifices were on a magnificent scale. It seems
probable that human beings were sacrificed as well as animals, but we have no definite
proof of this. So sudden and extensive a change, which involved so much trouble and
cost, can hardly be explained by anything but a new and strong interest in the after-life,
and a desire to provide the dead with everything needful in it. Possibly, as Shetelig
suggested,[^14-1|\*] the elaborate funerals of the Christian Merovingian kings filled the heathen
rulers of Norway with the desire to emulate them. But we do not find such rich graves in
Denmark, and the increase in grave-goods is not restricted to the upper classes, so that the
impression gained is that the supply of possessions to the dead had become a necessity in
heathen thought. Whether any new ideas could have come from the southeast, by way of
trade-routes through Russia, is perhaps a question worth considering. It is noteworthy that
in the tenth century we have a ship-funeral described on the Volga whose elaborate ceremonial
gives a picture like that suggested by the treasures of the Oseberg ship.

[^12-4]: For a summary of recent work on the subject see Almgren, *Nordische Felszeichnungen als religiose
Urkunden* (Frankfurt, 1934), p. 304 f.
[^13-1]: Ebert, II, p. 122 f.
[^13-2]: Inserted by Alfred in his translation of Orosius. Relevant extracts given in A. J. Wyatt, *Anglo-Saxon
Reader* (Cambridge, 1925), p. 15.
[^14-1]: *Osebergfundet* (Brøgger. Falk, Shetelig: Christiania, 1917), I, p. 249.
::::
::::translation{lang=zh-CN}
纵观这些丧葬习俗，某些做法的变化尤为显著，这表明关于来世和墓葬的新理念必然已影响到了那些确立这些习俗的人群。
首要且最引人注目的是青铜时代从土葬向火葬的转变。这种转变的动因何在？
是为了让死者的灵魂摆脱肉体这一沉重牢笼的束缚，还是出于在尸体腐烂前保护生者免受危害？
简而言之⸺这是源于对死者的爱、恐惧，抑或皆有之？
这种新习俗是否反映了一种新观念⸺即存在一个不受束缚的灵魂可以前往的彼岸世界？
这些问题历来备受探讨，人们援引世界各地的习俗来支持各种理论，但至今尚无定论[^12-4cn|\*]。
其中，将火葬与燔祭以及太阳神崇拜联系起来的证据，或许最具吸引力。
很可能这并非任何一种因素的缘故，而是多种因素共同作用的结果。
可以肯定的是，这种在整个欧洲范围内被普遍接纳的剧变，必然满足了各地人群对于死者最深层的愿望与信仰，同时也暗示了相距遥远的地区在丧葬习俗与信仰之间存在着紧密的联系。
仅凭考古发现无法揭示火葬对于其践行者的确切意义，至于文献资料能否提供进一步的线索，我们将在后文中探讨。

在铁器时代初期，丧葬仪式发生了另一项值得注意的变化：
当时出现了一种习俗，即保留火葬柴堆上的所有残留物，并将其一并埋入墓穴，而不试图将骨骼与随葬品分离开来[^13-1|\*]。
这种做法也许是出于疏忽，但也有可能恰恰相反，反映出一种更审慎的态度⸺力求不遗漏或丢弃任何物品，以免亡者遭受苦难，或因怠慢亡者而给生者招致灾祸。
一位曾造访<u>阿尔弗雷德</u>国王宫廷的旅行者记载[^13-2cn|\*]，爱沙尼亚人在更晚的时期也保持着这种对待火葬的严谨态度：
<q>爱沙尼亚人有一种习俗，</q>他说，<q>即各部落均实行火葬；若发现有未烧尽的骨头，当事人必须进行严重的赎罪。</q>

罗马时期土葬习俗的回归是否源于信仰的改变，这一问题则更扑朔迷离。
其或许是受古代世界影响而传入的南方风尚的一部分，且之所以能较容易地被接纳，是因为当时的火葬习俗已不再承载关于来世的明确观念。
无论如何，这两种习俗长期并存，且各地做法有别，正如后来的挪威那样。

最后值得注意的变化发生在异教时期的晚期，即维京时代；
当时，挪威和瑞典各地各阶层在选择随葬品时，突然摒弃了七、八世纪葬礼那种简约的风格，转而采用一种繁复奢华的新习俗。
死者不仅获赠尽可能齐全的个人物品、武器、工具及生活用品，还有牲畜被宰杀以作陪葬；
而在权贵人士的葬礼上，无论男女，随葬的珍宝与祭祀规模更是宏大惊人。
尽管有迹象表明当时可能存在人殉，但目前尚无确凿证据。
这种突然且广泛、又耗资费力的变化，很难用其他理由来解释，除了将其归因于人们对来世产生了浓厚的新兴兴趣，并希望为死者提供来世所需的一切。
或许，如谢特利格推测[^14-1|\*]，信奉基督教的墨洛温王朝诸王那极尽奢华的葬礼，可能激发了挪威异教统治者效仿的愿望；
然而，我们在丹麦并未发现此类奢华墓葬，且随葬品增多的现象也并非仅限于上层阶级。
由此可见，在当时的异教观念中，为死者提供各类物品已成为一种必需。
至于是否存在某种新观念经由俄罗斯境内的贸易路线从东南方传入，或许也是一个值得探讨的问题。
因为值得注意的是，十世纪史料记载的伏尔加河畔的一场船葬，其繁复的仪式，与奥塞贝格船葬的随葬珍宝所呈现的图景颇有类似之处。

[^12-4cn]: 近年来有关这一主题的工作的总结参见 <span lang="en-GB">Almgren, *Nordische Felszeichnungen als religiose Urkunden* (Frankfurt, 1934), p. 304 f.</span>
[^13-2cn]: 阿尔弗雷德在其奥罗修斯译本中插入了这段文字。相关摘录见：<span lang="en-GB">A. J. Wyatt, *Anglo-Saxon Reader* (Cambridge, 1925), p. 15.</span>
