:::::

:::::translation-pair
::::original{lang=en-GB}
At this point a brief survey of the evidence for human sacrifice may be helpful. It
seems to have been known during the Bronze Age; the evidence of two elaborate howe-burials from the late period is at the least highly suggestive. In <q>King Björn's Howe</q> at
Uppsala[^14-2|\*] the burnt remains of a man lay in a man-length tree coffin inside a barrow, and
outside the coffin were the unburnt bones of at least three other adult persons, one of
them certainly a woman. Here as well as the possibility of suttee we have the more
gruesome one of cannibalism, for one of the human bones was split lengthwise as though
to extract the marrow. The grave-goods enable the date of the interment to be assigned to
Period IV of the Bronze Age, although the man-length coffin holding the burnt remains
and the richness of the objects are a link with an earlier period. In the second howe, at
Seddin in Brandenburg,[^14-3|\*] the grave-goods suggest a similar date, and Period IV&ndash;V has
been agreed upon. In this case we have the cremated remains of a man in a fine bronze
urn contained in an outer one of clay, while two other clay urns held the remains of two
women, one between twenty and thirty and one younger. The suggestion of human
sacrifice given by these graves is strengthened by
a discovery at Vemmerlöv in Sweden,[^15-1|\*] in a peat bog. Two artificial pools, originally
enclosed with sharpened stakes, were found to hold the bones of many animals, and
human bones of at least four persons were included among them. The fact that only parts
of the larger animals were found suggests that the rest was consumed at a sacrificial meal,
and if this is true it seems that the same fate overtook the human beings also, for the
skeletons were not complete and their bones lay mixed with the rest. Although such a
find can hardly be dated accurately, examination of the soil proves it to be earlier than the
Iron Age, while the marks of metal weapons on the stakes argues against the Neolithic
period; and the fact that Bronze Age rock engravings are found nearby is an additional
reason for assigning it to the same period.

The sacrifice of animals on a large scale at the funeral is again found in Sweden at the
time of the Vendel graves, for the chieftains there were provided with many domestic
animals, horses, dogs, hawks and other birds. Again, in the Viking Age many animals,
and particularly horses, were buried with the dead, as the Gokstad and Oseberg ship-
burials show. We have, however, no conclusive evidence for human sacrifice. Two
women were apparently laid in the Oseberg ship, and it is possible that one of them may
have been a servant slain to accompany her mistress, but the disturbed state of the burial
makes it absolutely impossible to prove this. There are also a number of graves to be
taken into account where the grave-goods suggest a double interment, since in them
weapons and tools and characteristically masculine possessions are found mixed with
those generally associated with women's graves, weaving implements, ornaments and the
like. This might mean that occasionally in the Viking Age a widow was sacrificed at her
husband's funeral. Shetelig made a list of all such graves known in 1908,[^15-2|\*] and his results
are certainly suggestive; it may be noticed that all his examples are cremation ones, and
of Viking Age date, and it has since been pointed out[^15-3|\*] that they are mostly graves of the
tenth century. But Scandinavia has no definite evidence to equal that of the Gaulish
cemetery of the early Iron Age at Thuizy,[^15-4|\*] where, out of sixty-four graves, twenty-eight
contained double interments, with the man and woman
placed in a close embrace. The evidence as it stands is however worth taking into account
to compare with that of the literature later on.

[^14-2]: Ebert, VII, p. 133 f.
[^14-3]: *Ibid.* XI, p. 444 f.
[^15-1]: *Ibid.* XIV p. 112 f.
[^15-2]: *Viking Club Saga Book*, VI, 1909, p. 180 f.
[^15-3]: Engelstad, *Bergens Museums Aarbok*, III, r927, p. 60.
[^15-4]: Déchelette, *Manuel d'Archéologie* (Paris, 1908&ndash;1914), II, p. 1035 f.
::::
::::translation{lang=zh-CN}

此时，简要考察一下有关人​​祭的证据或许会有所助益。
这种习俗似乎在青铜时代就已存在，晚期两处精心营建的[冢墓]^(howl burial)所出土的证据强烈暗示了这一点。
在乌普萨拉的<u>比约恩王冢</u>中[^14-2|\*]，一座封土堆内发现了一具装有男性的火化遗骸的等身长木棺，棺外则散落着至少三名成年人的未火化骨骼，其中一人确认为女性。
除了可能存在的殉葬习俗之外，另一种可能性更为骇⸺食人，因为其中一根人骨呈纵向劈开状，似乎是为了取食骨髓。
随葬品表明该墓葬的年代属于青铜时代第四期，尽管容纳火化遗骸的等身长木棺以及丰富的随葬物品，都显示出与更早时期文化的联系。
在<u>勃兰登堡</u><u>塞丁</u>第二座墓的随葬品也指向相似的年代[^14-3|\*]，学界普遍定为青铜时代第四至五期。
该墓中，一名男性的火化遗骸被置于精美的青铜骨灰瓮内，外层再套以陶制骨灰瓮；
另有两个陶制骨灰瓮则各自盛放着两名女性的遗骸，一人年龄在二十至三十岁之间，另一人则更为年轻。
瑞典<u>韦默勒夫</u>[^15-1|\*]一处泥炭沼泽中的发现，进一步强化了这些墓葬所暗示的人祭习俗。
其中发现了两个人工水池，原先被削尖的木桩围起，池内不仅有大量动物骨骼，还混杂着至少四名人类的遗骸。
大型动物仅存部分骨骼，暗示其余部分可能在祭祀宴会上被食用。
而若此推断属实，其中人类可能也遭遇了同样的命运，因为这些人类骨骼并不完整，且与其他骨骼混杂在一起。
尽管此类遗迹难以精确断代，但土壤分析证实其年代早于铁器时代，而木桩上留下的金属武器痕迹则排除了新石器时代的可能性；
此外，附近发现的青铜时代岩刻，也为将其归入同一时期提供了佐证。

在瑞典的文德尔墓群中，再次出现了葬礼上大规模宰杀动物的习俗；
当时，首领的随葬品中包括许多家畜，如马、狗、鹰及其他鸟类。
同样，在维京时代，许多动物（尤其是马）也常与死者一同下葬，戈克斯塔和奥塞贝格的船葬便是明证。
然而，我们尚无人殉的确凿证据。
奥塞贝格号船葬墓内部确实安放了两名女性，其中一人可能是殉葬女主人的仆从，但由于墓葬遭到扰动，这一点已无法证实。
此外，还有若干墓葬的随葬品暗示了合葬的存在：这些墓中，武器、工具等典型的男性用品，与织布器具、饰品等通常见于女性墓葬的物品混杂在一起。
这或许意味着，在维京时代，有时会发生寡妇在丈夫葬礼上殉葬的情况。
谢特利格曾于1908年列出过所有此类已知墓葬的清单[^15-2|\*]，其研究结果颇具启发性。
值得注意的是，他所列举的案例均为火葬墓，且后有学者指出[^15-3|\*]，这些墓葬大多可追溯至十世纪的维京时代。
但是，在斯堪的纳维亚地区，我们并未发现能与铁器时代早期高卢人的<u>图伊齐</u>墓葬[^15-4|\*]相媲美的确凿证据：
在图伊齐墓地的六十四座墓葬中，有二十八座为合葬墓，男女死者被安放成紧密相拥的姿态。
尽管如此，现有的这些证据仍值得作为日后与文献记载进行比对的参考。
::::
:::::

:::::translation-pair
::::original{lang=en-GB}

<a id="chapter-1-funeral-ship"></a>

## The Funeral Ship

Archaeological records can supply nothing to bridge the gap in time between the shipgraves in Gotland at the beginning of the Iron Age and the graves which contain the buried or burnt remains of ships, for which we have evidence in Scandinavia from about A.D. 500. There is always the possibility that before this ships may have been made without rivets, as indeed is the case with one recovered from a bog,[^16-1|\*] and so could leave no trace when the wood decayed or after being burned; if this were the case, then shipfuneral in the North might go back earlier than our records. On the other hand, Lindqvist[^16-2|\*] has made the disquieting suggestion that the rivets found in the early graves are not necessarily from ships at all, and are more likely to come from great chests burned or buried with the dead. He will accept no example of ship-funeral as definite before that of the earliest of the Vendel graves, which he dates at about A.D. 600.

The graves at Vendel[^16-3|\*] certainly offer a striking example of ship funeral. Two out of the fourteen graves excavated were in too poor a condition for anything definite to be said, but in all except one of the others the contents were certainly placed within a ship. The lack of actual human remains is rather disappointing; in many cases these were completely missing, possibly because the grave had been plundered, while other graves contained only a few fragments of human bones. The only graves which held anything approaching a skeleton were number IX, thought to be of tenth-century date, where the remains of a man sitting upright on a chair were found, and one of the earliest of the graves, number XIV, where enough remained to show that the chieftain was again seated in full war-gear in the stern of the ship with his horse beside him. This is the grave which Lindqvist believes to be the oldest of all, and dates at 600 or a little earlier. Although the human remains are scanty, the graves held a large number of animal bones. Usually domestic animals&mdash;sheep, cattle, pigs&mdash;were found, some lying in the ship and some out; and there were a great many skeletons of horses, especially in the early graves, where a number were sometimes found in a row on the starboard side with their heads towards the prow. There were a number of dogs too, large and small, a few hawks, and at least one duck and one goose. The warriors seem to have been provided with all their weapons, and in the forepart of the ship there were cauldrons, cooking utensils and so on. In grave XIV the dead man had evidently been deliberately supplied with food, for instead of the usual whole skeletons of animals the remains of a joint, a ham and a sheep's head were found.

There were again remains of animals in the cremation graves of the kings at Uppsala, although Lindqvist contends,[^17-1|\*] on account of the size and shape of the rivets found, that no ship could have been burned there. The dead whose ashes rest in these graves appear to have been burned in a rude hut or burial chamber, whose supporting posts left clear traces when they burned to the ground. Lindqvist points out that there is a striking resemblance between this and the custom of setting up burial chambers on ships buried in the earth, as was done in the case of the three great ships of the Viking Age excavated in Norway.

[^16-1]: *Nordiske Fortidsminder*, in, I, p. 72 f. (<q>Hjortspringfundet</q>).
[^16-2]: Lindqvist, *Fornvännen*, 1921, p. 100 f.
[^16-3]: Stolpe and Arne, *La Nécropole de Vendel* (Stockholm, 1927), p. 8 f.
[^17-1]: Lindqvist, *Fornvännen*,1921, pp. 100 f., 187 f.

::::
::::translation{lang=zh-CN}

## 葬船

从铁器时代初期哥特兰岛的船形墓，到约公元500年斯堪的纳维亚地区出现的、包含或火化或直接掩埋的船只遗存的墓葬，考古记录无法填补这之间的空白。
一种可能性是，此前建造的船只未使用铆钉，例如一艘从沼泽中出土的船只那样[^16-1|\*]。
因此，一旦木材腐烂或船只被焚毁，便不会留下任何痕迹。
若果真如此，北欧的船葬习俗可能比现有记录显示的年代更为久远。
另一方面，<u>林德奎斯特</u>[^16-2|\*]提出了一个令人不安的观点：早期墓葬中发现的铆钉未必来自船只，而更有可能来自随死者一同焚烧或掩埋的大型箱柜。
在他看来，在文德尔墓群中年代最早的墓葬出现、也就是他定代的约公元600年之前，尚无确凿的船葬实例。

文德尔墓群[^16-3|\*]无疑是船葬习俗的一个显著例证。
在发掘出的十四座墓葬中，有两座因保存状况极差而无法得出确切结论，但在其余墓葬中，除一座外，所有随葬品都明显置于船体之内。
可惜的是，墓中几乎没有发现真正的人类遗骸；或因遭盗掘所致，许多墓葬中完全不见遗骸踪迹，另一些则仅存少量碎骨。
仅有两座墓葬保留了接近完整骨架的遗存：其一是9号墓，推测年代为10世纪，其中发现了一具端坐在椅子上的男性遗骸；
其二是年代最早的墓葬之一⸺14号墓，足以看出其中遗存的样貌：一位首领身披全套战甲，端坐在船尾，身旁伴有战马。
林德奎斯特认为这座墓葬年代最为久远，可追溯至公元600年或更早。
尽管人类遗骸稀少，这些墓葬中却出土了大量动物骨骼。
通常发现的都是家畜，如绵羊、牛、猪；有的位于船内，有的则在船外。
现场还发现了大量马匹的骨骼，尤其是在年代较早的墓葬中，有时可见数匹马沿船身右舷一字排开，头朝船首方向。
此外还有大大小小的狗、几只鹰，以及至少一只鸭子和一只鹅。
战士们似乎随葬了全部武器装备。而在船首部位，还放置着大锅、炊具等物品。
在第14号墓中，死者明显被特意供奉了食物，因为这里放置的不是通常的完整动物骨架，而是带骨肉块、火腿和羊头。

乌普萨拉诸王的火葬墓中同样发现了动物遗骸。
不过，林德奎斯特[^17-1|\*]根据所发现铆钉的尺寸与形状推断，此处并未焚烧过船只。
这些墓葬中安放的骨灰，似乎是在一座简陋小屋或墓室中火化得到的；当建筑被烧毁时，其支撑柱留下了清晰的痕迹。
林德奎斯特指出，这种做法非常类似<q>在埋入地下的船只上搭建墓室</q>的习俗，挪威出土的维京时代三艘大船便采用了这种葬制。

::::
:::::

:::::translation-pair
::::original{lang=en-GB}

These three ships, from Oseberg, Gokstad and Tuna, mark the culmination of the practice of ship-burial, and fortunately the nature of the soil and the fact that they were discovered at a late enough date to allow them to be scientifically moved and restored has preserved them for us with some of their contents. All three had been plundered, but enough of the grave-goods of the Oseberg and Gokstad ships remain to give an idea of the richness and elaborate ceremonial of the funeral. On each ship a burial chamber was set up, built roughly of timber, before the ship and its contents were covered with a large burial mound.

In the Oseberg ship[^17-2|\*] a great wealth of grave-goods remained, although the most valuable treasures and in particular the jewels and personal adornments of the woman buried there must have been removed by the robbers. The burial chamber had suffered most from their attentions, but some of its contents remained. There was a chest containing wild apples and corn and another full of small articles like iron clamps, combs and so on; the scattered remains of one or possibly two beds, with a quantity of soft material which very likely formed bed-coverings or hangings for the wall; four carved posts in the shape of animal heads, of beautiful and intricate workmanship; a number of buckets, one holding wild apples; and a stool and equipment for weaving. The after-part of the ship was fitted up like a kitchen and well equipped with cooking utensils, a grindstone and the like. In the fore-part there were various objects belonging to the ship, such as a gangway, tubs and bailers, and also an exquisite little wagon, with a bed laid on top, and four sledges. The wagon must from its position have been one of the first objects to be brought on to the ship, and since this and three of the sledges were of delicate and elaborate workmanship they were probably intended for ceremonial use only, and employed in the funeral procession when the bodies were brought on board. The remains of many animals were found in the ship; the body of an ox lay in the after-part, four dogs in the fore-ship, and beside the last a heap of horse skeletons, showing that at least ten animals must have been slain there. The heads of the horses and dogs had all been severed from their bodies, while the head of the ox was found, oddly enough, resting on the great bed in the fore-ship.

The ship was moored within the mound by a great stone, and a number of other stones had been thrown into her an around her before she was covered with earth. Yet in contrast to this, some of the oars had been placed in position, though others again lay to the east of the grave chamber and were not quite completed. The problem of who the dead woman was for whom this magnificent setting was prepared remains unsolved. The bones in the grave chamber had been ruthlessly scattered by the robbers.[^18-1|\*] Apparently two women had been laid there; one, whose skeleton was complete except for certain bones of the right hand and left arm, was a woman of between forty and fifty years of age. Of the other, few bones remained; parts of the skull suggest a younger woman of between twenty-five and thirty. The missing bones from the hand and arm of the one woman might well be accounted for by the fact that the robbers had carried them away along with the rings and bracelets which enriched them, but whether the other was so richly adorned that practically the whole body was taken is a question to which there seems little likelihood of finding an answer. Nor is it possible to say whether either of the two was a servant attending on her mistress, or whether this was a double burial of two members of the same family. No recognisable traces of clothes were found, except the sole of a shoe which the robbers may have dropped, a further pair of shoes in the grave chamber, and a sandal in the fore-part.

[^17-2]: *Osebergfundet* (Brøgger, Falk, Shetelig: Christiania, 1917), 1, p. 17 f.
[^18-1]: *Ibid.* 1, p. 147 f.

::::
::::translation{lang=zh-CN}

三艘大船，即奥塞贝格、戈克斯塔德和<u>图纳</u>三地出土的船只，标志着船葬习俗发展的巅峰。
得益于当地的土壤条件，加上发现时间较晚、得以进行科学的迁移与修复，这些船只及其部分随葬品得以有幸保存至今。
尽管这三艘船都曾遭洗劫，但奥塞贝格号和戈克斯塔德号残留的随葬品依然相当丰富，足以让人一窥当时葬礼的奢华与繁复仪式。
每艘船上都搭建有一间墓室，大约为木制，随后整艘船连同其中的物品都埋在了巨大的墓冢之下。

奥塞贝格号[^17-2|\*]上保留了大量的随葬品，尽管其中最珍贵的宝物已被盗墓者掠走，尤其是女性墓主的珠宝和个人饰品。
墓室最显眼，遭受的破坏也最严重，但仍有一些物品幸存下来。
其中包括一个装有野苹果和谷物的箱子，以及另一个装满铁扣、梳子等小物件的箱子；
还有一或两张床的零散残骸，附有大量柔软的织物，很可能是床罩或墙壁挂饰；
四根工艺精美繁复的兽头柱；若干个桶，其中一个盛有野苹果；以及一张凳子和一套织布工具。
船尾部分被布置成厨房的样子，配备了齐全的炊具、磨石等用品。
船头部分则存放着各种船具，如跳板、木盆和舀水器，还有一辆精致的、铺着床铺的小马车，以及四辆雪橇。
从摆放位置来看，这辆马车很可能是最早被搬上船的物品之一。
鉴于马车和其中三辆雪橇做工精细考究，它们很可能仅用于仪式场合，负责在葬礼游行中将遗体运送上船。
船内还发现了许多动物的遗骸：船尾处有一具牛的尸体，船头处有四只狗，狗的旁边则堆放着一堆马的骨架。
这表明当时至少有十只动物被宰杀献祭。
马和狗的头颅均与躯体分离，而牛的头颅却颇为离奇地安放在船首舱的大床上。

在被覆土掩埋之前，这艘船系在一块巨石上，船身内外还被投入了若干其他石块。
而与此形成对比的是，部分船桨已安放在位，另有一些则散落在墓室以东、未完全成型。
究竟是哪位女性享有了如此隆重的安葬规格，至今仍是个未解之谜。
墓室内的骸骨曾遭盗墓贼肆意散乱[^18-1|\*]。
显然，墓中曾安葬过两名女性：其中一人的骨架保存完好，除右手和左臂的几块骨头外，据推测生前年龄在四十至五十岁之间；
另一人的遗骨则所剩无几，仅存的颅骨碎片暗示她是一位二十五至三十岁左右的年轻女性。
前一位女性缺失的手臂骨骼，很可能是被盗墓贼连同佩戴的贵重戒指与手镯一并带走的；
至于另一位女性是否因佩戴了极其奢华的饰品、而导致几乎整具遗骸都被搬走，则恐怕永远无法查明真相了。
此外，也无法断定这两人中是否有一位是侍奉女主人的仆从，或是同一家族两名成员的合葬。
现场未发现任何可辨识的衣物痕迹，仅发现了一只可能是盗墓贼遗落的鞋底、墓室内的一双鞋、以及船首部位的一只凉鞋。
::::
:::::

:::::translation-pair
::::original{lang=en-GB}

In the Gokstad ship[^19-1|\*] no rich articles remained, except one sledge which may have been used at the funeral. There were a number of wooden fittings belonging to the ship, including a finely carved tiller, some kitchen vessels, a large copper cauldron, the remains of what seems to be a gaming board, and a few bronze buckles and ornaments from the clothes of the dead man. Remains of the skeleton indicate that the burial was that of a man of at least fifty years of age and possibly older, who had suffered from chronic rheumatism which must have made him practically a cripple at the time of death, and it has been suggested that he might be identified with Olaf of Geirstaðr, the king of Vestfold who died of <q>leg-pains</q> *(fótverkr)* about 840.[^19-2|\*] At least twelve horses and a number of dogs had been killed at the funeral, and were placed in the mound outside the ship, but the remains of a peacock were discovered inside. An interesting feature about this burial is that apparently deliberate damage had been done to some of the objects placed in the ship, such as cannot be attributed to the pillagers of the burial chamber. In particular the sledge had been broken into fragments, and these scattered about the ship.

Of the third ship-burial, from Tuna,[^19-3|\*] excavated as early as 1867, very little was preserved. The ship contained a cremation burial, and a horse seems to have been laid inside the grave chamber. It is thought from the report of these finds that the burial was that of a man, but practically nothing has survived. The grave had been robbed like the other two.

The use of the burial chamber in these three ships, in which, as we know from the Oseberg ship beds might be set up for the dead to rest on, contrasts with the Vendel graves, in which the practice seems to have been to set the dead warrior upright in the stern; so indeed does the practice of burying women in this way at all. The fact that the Oseberg ship was not an isolated case, however, is suggested by finds from Tuna, in the district of Alsike,[^19-4|\*] where a number of boat-graves from the Viking Age or a little earlier were excavated, and found to resemble in many ways the cemetery at Vendel, and the remains of women were found among them; unfortunately the state of the graves was very poor indeed, and the earth had been much disturbed, so that details about the individual graves are uncertain. As far as could be ascertained, the dead lay or sat in their ships as in the Vendel graves.

Lindqvist is anxious to establish a connection between the burial chamber in the three big ship-burials discussed above and the rough chambers supported on wooden posts in which the dead seem to have been burned at the graves at Old Uppsala. Apart from this feature, however, the personal possessions round the body, the kitchen utensils in the after-part of the ship, and the number of animals slain-some inside the ship and others out&mdash;are characteristic also of the Vendel and Tuna graves, though the beheading of the animals appears to be a new practice.

Besides Scandinavia proper, in which as long ago as 1906 Shetelig[^20-1|\*] reckoned in four figures the number of graves containing ships, there is evidence for the same practice in lands colonised from Norway and Sweden in the Viking Age. Burnt vessels are found in graves in Finland from the seventh century;[^20-2|\*] they have been recorded from Swedish settlements in North Russia,[^20-3|\*] and several funeral ships have been found in Iceland, although none of these graves is on such an elaborate scale as those which have previously been described. Strangely enough there has been little evidence for shipfuneral from Denmark, although in 1935 a rich burial in a ship was excavated at Ladby on the Isle of Fyen near Odense.[^20-4|\*] Certain features recall the great Norwegian burials, for the ship held the skeletons of eleven horses and some dogs with their trappings. The body was missing, and some of the richest and most interesting grave-goods had probably been removed, but a piece of bronze coupling for leashing the dogs was decorated in the Jellinge style of ornament which flourished in the early tenth century. In Brittany a large barrow containing the grave of a chief burned in his ship was excavated about 1909,[^20-5|\*] and proved to hold a large number of grave-goods and some animal bones; it is thought from the human remains&mdash;in poor condition&mdash;that it may have held two persons. A smaller ship-burial, which contained the remains of a horse, was found in the Isle of Man in 1928.[^21-1|\*] On the whole however the Viking settlements in the British Isles have produced disappointingly little evidence for ship-funeral, and only a small number of graves in the Hebrides and the Orkneys contain rivets which indicate that ships were burned or buried there.[^21-2|\*]

[^19-1]: N. Nicolaysen, *Laugskibet fra Gokstad* (Christiania, 1882).
[^19-2]: *Ibid*. p. 70.
[^19-3]: Montelius, <q>Högsätting i skepp under Vikingatiden</q>, *Svensk fornminnesfören tidskrift*, VI.
[^19-4]: Stolpe, <q>Tuna-fyndet</q>, *Ymer*,1895, p. 219 f.
[^20-1]: Shetelig, *Viking Club Saga Book*, IV, 1906, p. 326 f.
[^20-2]: Hackrnan, *Om likbränning i batar... i Finland* (Finskt Museum, 1897).
[^20-3]: Arne, *La Suède* et *l'Orient* (Uppsala, 1914), pp. 32&ndash;33.
[^20-4]: E. M. Hardy, *Mariner's Mirror*, XXV, a, April (Review of original report).
[^20-5]: Chatellier and Pontois, *Viking Club Saga Book*, VI, 1909, p. 123 f.
[^21-1]: Kermode, *Journal of Society of Antiquaries*, 1930, p. 126 f.
[^21-2]: *Ibid. p*.133.

::::
::::translation{lang=zh-CN}

戈克斯塔德号[^19-1|\*]上没有留下任何贵重物品，除了一个可能用于葬礼的雪橇外。
船上有一些木制配件，包括一个雕刻精美的舵柄、一些厨房用具、一个大铜锅、一些疑似棋盘的残骸，以及一些死者衣物上的青铜扣环和饰品。
骨骼残骸表明墓主人至少五十岁，甚至可能更老。
他生前患有慢性风湿病，死时几乎已经瘫痪。
有人认为他可能是<u>盖尔斯塔德的奥拉夫</u>，即公元840年左右死于[腿痛]^(fótverkr)[^19-2|\*]的<u>韦斯特福尔德</u>国王。
葬礼上至少有十二匹马和数只狗被宰杀，并置于船外的土堆中，而船内则发现了一只孔雀的遗骸。
这座墓葬有意思的一点是，船上的一些物品显然遭到了蓄意破坏，且并非墓室盗墓者所为。
尤其是雪橇被砸成碎片，散落在船内各处。

第三座船葬位于图纳[^19-3|\*]，早在1867年就已发掘，但保存下来的遗物寥寥无几。
船内为火葬墓，墓室中似乎放置了一匹马。
根据这些发现的记载，人们推测墓主人是一位男性，但基本没有任何证据留存。
与其他两条船一样，这座墓也遭到了盗掘。

可以从奥塞贝格船葬中得知，这三座船葬中的墓室中可能设有供死者安息的床铺，其用途与文德尔墓葬群形成对比：
一是文德尔时期的习俗似乎是将死去的战士竖立放置在船尾；二是文德尔根本没有船葬的女性。
然而，奥塞贝格船葬并非女性船葬孤例⸺<u>阿尔西克区</u>的图纳[^19-4|\*]发掘出许多维京时代或稍早时期的船葬墓，它们和文德尔墓群多有相似之处，并且其中还发现了女性遗骸；
可惜的是，这些墓葬保存状况极差，土层遭到严重扰动，因此关于单个墓葬的细节尚不清楚。
能弄清楚的部分表明，死者和文德尔墓葬中的死者一样，是躺或坐在船中的。

林德奎斯特立刻就把以上三座大型船葬墓室，与老乌普萨拉墓地中用木柱支撑、用来焚烧死者的简陋墓室联系起来。
除了这一特征外，尸体周围的个人物品、船尾的厨房用具以及船内外被宰杀的动物数量，也是文德尔和图纳墓葬的共同特征，虽然文德尔的动物没有被斩首。

早在1906年，谢特利格[^20-1|\*]就统计出斯堪的纳维亚半岛本土有四位数的墓葬中包含船只。
除此地区以外，维京时代的挪威和瑞典殖民地也有类似的证据。
芬兰的墓葬中发现了公元7世纪的烧毁船只[^20-2|\*]；北俄罗斯的瑞典人定居点也发现了烧毁船只[^20-3|\*]；
冰岛也发现了数艘葬船，尽管规模都不及之前描述的那些墓葬。
奇怪的是，丹麦鲜有船葬的证据，不过1935年在<u>奥登塞</u>附近<u>菲恩岛</u>的<u>拉德比</u>发掘出了一处豪华船葬[^20-4|\*]。
某些特征令人联想起挪威的大型墓葬，因为船上发现了十一匹马、一些狗的骨骼及其装备。
其尸体已遗失，最宝贵珍奇的陪葬品大概已被盗走，但一块用于拴狗的青铜链环留了下来，其纹饰是十世纪初盛行的<u>耶灵</u>风格。
布列塔尼1909年左右发掘出一座大型墓冢，其中埋葬着一位在船上被火化的首领[^20-5|\*]，
墓冢中发现了大量陪葬品和一些动物骨骼；根据保存状况不佳的人类遗骸推测，墓中可能埋葬着两人。
1928 年，<u>马恩岛</u>发现了一处较小的船葬，其中包含一匹马的遗骸[^21-1|\*]。
然而，总体而言，不列颠群岛的维京人定居点的船葬证据少得令人失望，只有<u>赫布里底群岛</u>和<u>奥克尼群岛</u>的少数墓葬中发现了铆钉，表明那里曾焚烧或埋葬过船只[^21-2|\*]。

::::
:::::

:::::translation-pair
::::original{lang=en-GB}

Examples of ship-funeral from an earlier period have however been found in South-East England. The first discovery was that of a ship buried in a tumulus at Snape Common, near Aldeburgh.[^21-3|\*] When a group of barrows was excavated here in 1862, the remains of a clinker-built boat about 48 ft. long was found in the undisturbed soil under the largest barrow. Nothing more was found inside, unfortunately, than two masses of what was thought to be human hair, fragments of a green glass goblet of early Anglo-Saxon style, and a gold ring of the late Roman period. The glass and the ring made an early date in the Anglo-Saxon period probable. and the burial was therefore thought to be of sixth-century or even fifth-century date. This made it stand absolutely alone, since no other ship-burial was recorded in the British Isles before the Viking Age. However in the summer of 1939 this early dating was to a large extent confirmed by the spectacular discovery made at Sutton Hoo, near Woodbridge in Suffolk. A number of mounds stand on a high piece of ground above the river Deben, and in 1938 two of the smaller ones were excavated, when it was found that one contained traces of a boat 18 to 20 ft. long, with a rounded stern. Only a few gold fragments were left inside, and evidently the goods had been removed. The second mound held cremated remains, with associated objects suggesting a sixth- or seventh-century date, but insufficient to allow definite conclusions to be drawn. The third and largest of the group of mounds was opened in the summer of the following year, and this revealed a truly astonishing find, an Anglo-Saxon ship-burial on a royal scale.[^21-4|\*] The vessel in the mound had been dragged up from the river some half mile away and lowered into its resting-place from above, like a coffin; although the wood had rotted away, the outline could be clearly seen, and was that of a clinker-built boat, for thirty eight rowers, with a length of 85 ft. A burial chamber had been placed amidships, built quite roughly of oak boards and with a gable-ended roof probably ridged with turf. An attempt made to rob the barrow had providentially failed owing to the depth at which the ship was buried, and the only damage to the contents was that caused by the fall of the roof of the burial chamber many years after the burial, when the weight of the sand above was too much for it. Thus we have an almost unique opportunity to examine a ship-burial on a grand scale, in fairly good condition, and undisturbed by human agency from the day when it was laid in the earth.

The grave-goods were found lying on the floor of the chamber, though possibly some may have been hanging on the wall before it collapsed. There were a great number of very varied objects. Some seemed as though they must have formed part of the ceremonial regalia of a king; there was, for example, the boss of a mighty shield, too huge for normal use; a vast iron object decorated with bulls' heads, which may have been a portable flambeau intended to be stuck in the ground or else a symbolic structure to be borne before the king; and a great whetstone with each end decorated with strange human heads, beautifully carved in low relief. There were also weapons: a sword in its sheath, a magnificent helmet with a bronze face-piece and enriched with gold and silver, an ironbladed weapon of some kind, an iron axe, and a number of other axes, angons and socketed spear-heads thrust through the handle of a bronze bowl. There was also some chain mail, in very bad condition. Among the other treasures there were a set of nine silver bowls, seven of them excellently preserved; two silver spoons and a number of horns with silver mounts, crushed beyond restoration hut still recognisable; a great dish of silver of Byzantine workmanship, with a smaller one beneath it; six bottles made from gourds; a silver dipper and small bowl; and two bronze hanging bowls, perhaps fallen from the roof. There was also a wooden tub, and three bronze cauldrons and iron tackle to support them. Finally, there were what might be called personal possessions: a small musical instrument, a mysterious little tray of fine wood decorated with garnets, two rich gold clasps in cloisonné work which might have been intended for the shoulders of a cuirass, the gold frame of a purse, two buckles, some gold plaques, a strap-end, and other small objects of a similar kind. All these ornaments, except for the largest buckle, lay in a position which suggested that they had been hung up on the walls. A large leather bag with a silver handle and another smaller one were found among a mass of remains of textiles and leather, and finally there were forty Merovingian gold coins which had fallen out of the purse.

[^21-3]: Smith, *Victoria County History of Suffolk*, p. 325.
[^21-4]: *Antiquity*, March 1940, <q>The Sutton Hoo Ship-burial</q>.

::::
::::translation{lang=zh-CN}

不过，在英格兰东南部也发现了更早期的船葬遗迹。
最早的发现是在<u>奥尔德堡</u>附近的<u>斯内普公地</u>，1862年发掘出来的一批古坟中，其中最大的一座下方的原状土层中，发现了一艘长约[48英尺]^(14.6m)、采用[^tr-clinker|叠板法]建造的船只遗骸[^21-3|\*]。
遗憾的是，墓内除了一些被认为是人发的团块、几块早期盎格鲁-撒克逊风格的绿色玻璃高脚杯碎片，以及一枚罗马晚期的金戒指外，别无他物。
这些玻璃器皿和戒指暗示该墓葬年代可能处于盎格鲁-撒克逊时期早期，因此推断其属于六世纪甚至五世纪。
这一定代时间乃是孤例，因为不列颠群岛从未有过其他维京时代前船葬的记录。
然而，1939年夏，<u>萨福克郡</u><u>伍德布里奇</u>附近的<u>萨顿胡</u>出土的惊人遗迹，很大程度上证实了此前墓葬年代的推断。
1938年，人们发掘了<u>德本河</u>畔的高地上分布的数座土丘其中较小的两座。
其中一座内留有长[18至20英尺]^(5.5-6m)、船尾呈圆形的船只痕迹，其内部仅存少量金质碎片⸺随葬品显然已被盗走。
第二座土丘内发现了火葬遗骸及相关器物，虽暗示其年代为六或七世纪，但证据不足以得出确切结论。
次年夏天，该墓群中最大的一座土丘被发掘，而其中埋藏着惊喜：一座具有王室规格的盎格鲁-撒克逊船葬[^21-4|\*] 。
埋于土丘中的这艘长85英尺、设有38名桨手的叠板式船只，最初是从约[半英里]^(800m)外的河边被拖运上来的，随后像安放棺椁一样，自上方吊入安息之所。
尽管其木材早已腐朽，船体的轮廓依然清晰可见。
船身中部设有一处墓室，由橡木板粗略搭建而成，配有人字形屋顶，顶部或曾覆有草皮。
曾有人试图盗掘这座古冢，但因船只埋藏极深而未能得逞。
墓内物品遭受的唯一损坏，源于下葬多年后、上方沙土重压导致墓室屋顶坍塌。
因此，这是一个难得的契机，来考察一处规模宏大、保存状况相当完好，且自入土以来未受人为扰动的船葬遗迹。

被发现时，随葬品散落在墓室地面上，虽然其中一些在墓室坍塌前或曾挂在墙壁上。
这些物品种类繁多，数量惊人。
有些似乎属于国王的礼仪法器，例如：
一面巨大盾牌的盾毂，尺寸过大不便常规使用；
一件饰有公牛头图案的巨大铁制器物，可能是某种插入地面使用的便携式火炬架，也可能是国王出巡时由人持举在前的象征物；
还有一块巨大的磨刀石，其两端雕刻着造型奇特的人头浮雕，工艺精湛。
此外还有各类武器：
一把入鞘的宝剑、一顶配有青铜面甲及金银装饰的华丽头盔、一件铁刃武器、一把铁斧，以及其他几把斧头、倒钩长矛和插在青铜碗把手上的带銎矛头。
现场还发现了一些保存状况极差的锁子甲。
其他珍宝包括一套九只银碗（其中七只保存完好）、两把银勺、若干镶有银饰的兽角（虽已严重压损无法修复，但仍可辨认）、一个拜占庭风格的大银盘（下方叠放着一个小银盘）、六个葫芦制成的瓶、一把银长柄勺和一个小银碗，以及两个青铜悬挂碗（可能是从屋顶掉落的）。
此外还有一只木桶、三口青铜大锅及其铁制支架。
最后是一些可归类为个人物品的物件：
一件小型乐器；一个用优质木材制成并镶嵌石榴石的神秘小托盘；两个精美的掐丝珐琅金饰扣（可能用于胸甲肩部）；一个钱袋的金质框架；两个带扣；几块金饰片；一个皮带末端饰件；以及其他类似的小物件。
除最大的带扣外，所有这些饰物的位置都表明它们原本是悬挂在墙壁上的。
在一堆纺织品和皮革残骸中，发现了两个皮包，其中一个配有银质提手；最后还发现了四十枚从钱袋中散落出来的墨洛温王朝金币。

[^tr-clinker]: 译注：指北欧发源的、船体长方向木板间有重叠、并用金属紧固件固定的造船工艺。

::::
:::::

:::::translation-pair
::::original{lang=en-GB}

The most important of the grave-goods and all the personal equipment were placed at the west end of the burial chamber. At the east end were the cauldrons and various household vessels, while objects like the dishes and the horns lay between the two. The body might have been expected to lie at the west end of the chamber, but absolutely no trace of human remains of any kind were discovered, and there were no signs of burned bones or of any vessel that could have held cremated remains; nor did the position of the buckles, helmet and so on give the impression that they might have been placed on a dead man. It is not the first time that a grave has been found mysteriously empty; a number of the Vendel graves contained no human remains, and none were found in the Danish ship burial at Fyen or in the ship excavated at Snape Common.[^23-1|\*] However, in these cases either the grave had been entered by robbers or else was in such a poor condition that no definite conclusions could be reached as to whether the body had been there or not. The grave at Sutton Hoo, however, had never been entered from the time when it was closed at the funeral, and the archaeologists who examined it are confident that human remains could not have disappeared without leaving some trace. It has been suggested that the mound may have been erected as a cenotaph, in memory of a king who was lost at sea; and perhaps in view of the other graves left mysteriously without an occupant there may be more Scandinavian examples of the same practice than have been previously recognised. Certainly one definite example is recorded from the Farmanns barrow in Norway, probably erected as a monument for one or more members of the Vestfold royal family to which King Harald the Fair-haired belonged. Brøgger, in his account of the grave, mentions other examples of howes in Scandinavia in which no human remains have been found.[^23-2|\*] Among the graves from the Christian period excavated in the Viking settlement in Greenland one was found to contain a stick carved in runes, which appears to have been buried to represent the dead person, whose body had been lost at sea.[^23-3|\*]
Mr. Phillips has pointed out that another example of an elaborate burial from Saxon times, the Broomfield grave, seems from the records to have been empty of human remains, since the carbon and soot then thought to indicate a possible cremation are more likely to be traces of decayed woodwork. Another very puzzling feature of the Sutton Hoo burial was the basin of rough clay which seems to have been placed above the roof of the burial chamber, and which it is suggested may have been intended for libations.

The evidence of the finds dates the grave in the early part of the seventh century. The numismatists are disposed from the evidence of the coins to select a date about the middle of the century, or at any rate after 630. But such late dating is most improbable, since we know that by 640 Christianity was well established in East Anglia, and a burial such as this would necessitate a reversion to heathen customs for which we have no evidence. If a date between 600 and 640 is accepted, it seems probable that the cenotaph, if cenotaph it is, is that of Redwald, who ruled East Anglia from about 593 to 617, and was the only one of its kings to hold the title of High King of Britain, which belonged to him for some years before he died. This is Chadwick's suggestion,[^24-1|\*] supported by Bede's picture of the king as a man who never wholly gave up his heathen beliefs, and by the probability that Rendlesham, only four miles from this group of barrows, was Redwald's palace. It seems likely that a king with well-known tendencies towards the old faith, and who moreover was survived by a wife who supported it with enthusiasm, was the person in whose honour the barrow was raised; and this is rendered more probable by the fact that at the end of his reign he must have been the richest king in England.[^24-2|\*]

The barrows at Sutton Hoo, two of which have proved to be ship-graves, evidently formed part of a cemetery which goes back into the heathen period, and of which the chief grave has remained, miraculously, untouched. Excavation of the remaining barrows may throw more light on the date of the treasure. But we have undoubtedly here an example of an elaborate ship-burial in Anglo-Saxon times in the kingdom of East Anglia, corresponding in date with that of the Vendel graves in Sweden. Here, moreover, the erection of a burial chamber above the ship provides a parallel to the later ship-graves of the Viking period, and probably too with the burial chamber of which traces have been left in the Uppsala gravemounds. The barrows at Snape, where the other ship-burial was found, indicate a second cemetery a little farther along the East Anglian coast; if the earlier date suggested for this burial is correct, we have ship-funeral in the British Isles as early as any recorded up to the present in Scandinavia. Shetelig's emphasis on the close cultural link between England and Scandinavia is confirmed by the treasure of Sutton Hoo, and the resemblance between it and the objects from the Vendel graves will probably become increasingly clear when the finds have been restored and examined further.

[^23-1]: It is likely that the mass thought to be human hair may have been animal fur of some kind (Phillips, *Antiquity, op. cit.* p. 6).
[^23-2]: A. V. Brøgger, *A.f.n.O.* 1921, p. 117 f.
[^23-3]: P. Norlund, Viking *Settlers in Greenland* (Cambridge, 1936), p. 51 f.
[^24-1]: Chadwick, *Antiquity, op. cit.* p. 76 f.
[^24-2]: Bede, *Historia Ecciesiastica* (ed. Plummer, vol. 1), II, ; III, 22; II, 5.

::::
::::translation{lang=zh-CN}

最重要的陪葬品及所有个人装备被放置在墓室西端，东端放有大釜和各类生活器皿，而盘子和角杯等物品则位于两者之间。
按理说，遗体应安放在墓室西端，但现场完全未发现任何人类遗骸的踪迹⸺既无焚烧过的骨骼，也无盛放火化遗骸的容器。
此外，带扣、头盔等物品的位置也不像是曾穿戴在死者身上的样子。
这并非首次发现神秘空置的墓葬；文德尔墓群中许多也未发现人类遗骸，丹麦菲恩岛和斯内普公地发掘出的船葬遗迹中也同样未见遗骸[^23-1cn|\*]。
然而，上述案例中，墓葬要么曾遭盗墓者洗劫，要么保存状况极差，从而无法确切判断其中是否曾安放过遗体。
相比之下，萨顿胡的这座墓葬自封墓以来从未开启过。
负责勘查的考古学家确信，若曾有遗体存在，绝不可能完全消失而不留任何痕迹。
有人推测，这座土丘可能是一座衣冠冢，以纪念一位在海上遇难的国王；
鉴于还有其他墓葬也呈现出这种神秘空置的状态，此类习俗在斯堪的纳维亚或许比此前认知的更为普遍。
挪威<u>法尔曼斯</u>古冢便是一个确凿的例子，它很可能是为纪念金发王哈拉尔所属的韦斯特福尔德王室的一位或多位成员而建的纪念碑。
<u>布勒格</u>在描述该墓葬时，还列举了斯堪的纳维亚地区其他未发现人类遗骸的古冢案例[^23-2|\*]。
在格陵兰维京人聚落发掘出的基督教时期墓葬中，有一座墓内仅埋有一根刻有卢恩字母的木棍；
这根木棍似乎是当作死者的象征埋葬的，而死者本人的遗体则已流落海中[^23-3|\*]。
菲利普斯先生还指出<u>布鲁姆菲尔德</u>墓，这一撒克逊时期的精美墓葬从记录上看似乎也空无一人；因为当时被认为是火葬迹象的碳和烟灰，更有可能是腐朽木制品的痕迹。
萨顿胡墓葬的另一个令人费解的特征是，墓室顶部似乎放置了一个粗糙的黏土盆，一说这是用来举行祭祀仪式的。

出土文物表明萨顿胡墓葬的年代可追溯至七世纪早期。
根据钱币的证据，钱币学家倾向于定代七世纪中期，或者至少在公元630年之后。
但如此晚的年代极不可能，因为众所周知，到公元640年，基督教已经在<u>东盎格利亚</u>地区广泛传播，而像这样的墓葬意味着当时人们重新回归了异教习俗，而我们没有任何证据可以佐证。
如果定在公元600年至640年间，那么这座衣冠冢（如果它确实是衣冠冢的话）很可能属于<u>雷德沃尔德</u>。
雷德沃尔德自大约公元593年至617年间统治东盎格利亚，是东盎格利亚国王中唯一一位拥有<q>不列颠至高王</q>称号的君主，是他去世前几年前获得的。
以上是查德威克的观点[^24-1|\*]，与之相佐证，<u>比德</u>也描述这位国王从未完全放弃异教信仰；而距离墓冢仅四英里的<u>伦德尔沙姆</u>很可能是雷德沃尔德的宫殿。
一位众所周知虔信古老信仰的国王，其遗孀也热情地支持他的信仰，那这座豪华墓冢很可能就是为了纪念他而建造的。
而雷德沃尔德统治末期也必然是英格兰最富有的国王，这更增加了这种可能性[^24-2|\*]。

萨顿胡的墓丘，其中两座已被证实为船葬，其所在的墓地显然可以追溯至异教时期，而其中最主要的墓穴奇迹般地保持完好。
对其余墓丘的挖掘或许能为宝藏的年代提供更多线索，但毫无疑问，我们已有盎格鲁-撒克逊时期东盎格利亚王国一处精美船葬的实例，其年代接近瑞典文德尔墓群。
此外，在船体上方建造墓室的做法，可以与维京时期的船葬、甚至乌普萨拉墓丘中的墓室遗迹相呼应。
斯内普另一处船葬遗址的墓丘表明，沿东盎格利亚海岸稍远的地方可能存在第二处墓地。
假如这一处墓葬年代确实更早，那么不列颠群岛船葬的出现时间，不会晚于迄今为止发现的任何斯堪的纳维亚船葬。
谢特利格强调英格兰与斯堪的纳维亚之间密切的文化联系，而萨顿胡宝藏的发现也印证了这一点。
随着进一步修复和研究，萨顿胡宝藏与文德尔出土文物之间的相似之处或将越发明晰。

[^23-1cn]: 此前被认为是人类毛发的物质或许是动物毛皮 (<span lang="en-GB">Phillips, *Antiquity, op. cit.* p. 6</span>).
